# Andrés Manuel López Obrador — Mexico, 2023

## 1. Overview

Andrés Manuel López Obrador (AMLO) was Mexico’s constitutional president from 1 December 2018 to 30 September 2024 and the dominant figure in the governing MORENA coalition. In 2023 he controlled the federal executive, set the political agenda through daily press conferences, and retained high approval. MORENA and allied parties held a congressional majority but lacked the supermajority required for constitutional amendments, while courts, autonomous electoral institutions, state governments, criminal organizations, economic constraints, and the United States limited his practical power.

His “Fourth Transformation” emphasized anti-corruption, social transfers, state-led infrastructure, energy sovereignty, and the declared reduction of violence. In 2023, the government expanded military responsibilities, defended major infrastructure projects, pursued an electoral reform later invalidated by the Supreme Court, and claimed declining homicide rates. Evidence also indicates persistent criminal territorial control, high impunity, risks to journalists and activists, institutional weakening, contested management of disappearances, and major infrastructure delays and cost increases.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record AMLO-2023-01 — Executive dominance constrained by Congress, courts, and criminal power

**Fact and significance.** AMLO exercised unusually strong agenda-setting power over the federal government and his party, but the 2021 legislative elections removed MORENA’s constitutional supermajority. Criminal groups also controlled or influenced some municipalities and regions, limiting the state’s effective authority. The Bertelsmann Stiftung reported that MORENA and allies retained 278 of 500 Chamber seats, enough for ordinary legislation but not constitutional reform; it also identified criminal organizations as a major constraint on governing power.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 2, 8–10. [Direct PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf). Relevant passages: executive summary and sections “Political Participation” and “Effective Power to Govern.”

**Period/status.** Mainly inherited and ongoing during 2023; the legislative constraint originated in 2021.

**Ruler connection.** Direct. The balance of congressional power and AMLO’s relationship with MORENA directly shaped his ability to enact his program.

**Complications.** AMLO retained high approval and expanded MORENA’s subnational reach; congressional majorities allowed substantial ordinary legislation.

**Cautions.** BTI is an expert comparative assessment, not a court record or administrative dataset. Its review period ends 31 January 2023.

**Independent support.** The Supreme Court’s 2023 decisions on electoral and National Guard legislation document institutional checks. [SCJN electoral-reform communiqué](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-02 — Military expansion into internal security and civilian administration

**Fact and significance.** During AMLO’s administration, the armed forces acquired extensive roles in public security, customs, ports, infrastructure, and administration of major projects. The National Guard, formally a civilian institution, was placed under operational control of the Defense Ministry by legislation; the Supreme Court later invalidated this transfer.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 2, 8–10. [PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf), especially lines describing National Guard control, customs, and military economic roles.

**Period/status.** Ongoing in 2023; some institutional changes were enacted in 2022 and reviewed in 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Direct. The transfer reflected an explicit presidential security and administrative strategy.

**Complications.** The government presented military participation as necessary for security, infrastructure delivery, and protection against organized crime. The Supreme Court’s intervention demonstrates that civilian constitutional checks remained operative.

**Cautions.** BTI uses evaluative language, including the possibility of future military threat, which is an assessment rather than an established outcome.

**Independent support.** The Supreme Court record identifies the challenge to the 2022 legislation concerning the National Guard and national security. [SCJN judicial record](https://sjf2.scjn.gob.mx/detalle/voto/46185).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-03 — Persistent criminal violence and limited state territorial control

**Fact and significance.** Mexico remained affected by organized criminal violence, territorial competition, and high impunity. BTI reported more than 30,000 murder victims for the fifth consecutive year through 2022, cartel control or strong influence in several states, and 97% of crimes going unpunished. These conditions continued into the 2023 period and complicated AMLO’s promise to address violence through social policy and a less militarized strategy.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 5–6. [PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf), “Monopoly on the Use of Force.”

**Period/status.** Inherited condition continuing into 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Moderate to strong. Violence predated AMLO, but security strategy and federal institutional choices were within presidential authority.

**Complications.** Mexico’s public-security ministry reported that intentional homicide declined 20% between the beginning of AMLO’s government and December 2023. [Mexican security ministry release](https://www.gob.mx/sspc/prensa/disminuye-20-homicidio-doloso-355856).

**Cautions.** Government homicide comparisons may depend on the chosen baseline and administrative classification. BTI’s impunity estimate is an external assessment and requires checking against Mexican justice statistics.

**Independent support.** ACLED’s Mexico country materials and its 2024 conflict-watchlist profile should be used for event-level violence and territorial patterns.

---

### Record AMLO-2023-04 — Disappearances and disputed official counting

**Fact and significance.** Mexico’s large population of missing and disappeared people remained a central public-safety and human-rights issue. In 2023 the government launched a generalized search strategy and revised the official registry, while critics feared that changes in verification and classification could reduce the apparent number of missing persons rather than resolve cases.

**Strongest source.** Mexican Ministry of the Interior, *El Gobierno de México presenta resultados sobre búsqueda y localización de personas desaparecidas*, 2023. [Government release](https://www.gob.mx/segob/prensa/el-gobierno-de-mexico-presenta-resultados-sobre-busqueda-y-localizacion-de-personas-desaparecidas).

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Direct. The search strategy was presented at a presidential morning conference and implemented by federal authorities.

**Complications.** The government reported searches and located individuals; these activities may represent genuine administrative improvement even if registry revisions were contested.

**Cautions.** The government source describes official actions and results but does not independently validate all findings. AP reporting should be opened directly for criticism concerning undercounting and registry manipulation.

**Independent support.** Human Rights Watch reported at least 105,000 officially registered disappeared persons in its Mexico country chapter. [HRW Mexico report](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2023/country-chapters/mexico).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-05 — Electoral “Plan B” weakened oversight and was invalidated

**Fact and significance.** AMLO’s administration promoted electoral reforms that would reduce the National Electoral Institute’s resources, personnel, and administrative capacity. The Supreme Court invalidated the legislative package on procedural grounds, restoring the prior electoral rules.

**Strongest source.** Supreme Court of Justice, communiqué on the invalidation of the electoral reform package, 2023. [SCJN communiqué](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408). It states that the bills were discussed and approved without being properly published to legislators and that the previous rules therefore resumed effect.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Direct. The reform was a central presidential legislative initiative.

**Complications.** The administration argued that the reform improved democratic representation and reduced bureaucratic excess. The president’s original constitutional proposal had already failed to obtain the required congressional majority.

**Cautions.** The cited judicial communiqué primarily establishes the Court’s legal reasoning, not the complete practical effects of the proposed reform.

**Independent support.** BTI described the reform as reducing INE resources and impairing electoral oversight. The INE itself warned that the changes would erode democracy, though its institutional interest must be considered. [INE statement](https://centralelectoral.ine.mx/2023/01/11/modificaciones-electorales-erosionan-la-democracia-son-inconstitucionales-y-regresan-la-intromision-del-gobierno-en-la-organizacion-de-comicios-lorenzo-cordova).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-06 — Pressure on journalists, activists, and critics

**Fact and significance.** Formal media freedom persisted, but journalists faced lethal violence, intimidation, and impunity, especially when reporting on crime, corruption, and links between political and criminal actors. AMLO also frequently attacked critical journalists and civil-society organizations in public statements.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 9–10. [PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf). BTI reports more than 168 journalist assassinations and 28 disappearances since 2006, with approximately 90% of cases unpunished.

**Period/status.** Inherited structural condition continuing during 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Moderate. Criminal organizations were responsible for many attacks, but federal protection, investigation, public rhetoric, and accountability were within government authority.

**Complications.** The federal government maintained formal press protections and operated a protection mechanism. Some criticism concerned state and criminal actors rather than direct presidential censorship.

**Cautions.** BTI’s cumulative totals and attribution should be checked against ARTICLE 19, CPJ, RSF, and Mexican official records.

**Independent support.** ARTICLE 19’s Mexico report documents attacks and structural conditions affecting journalism. [ARTICLE 19 report](https://articulo19.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/Resumen-Ejecutivo-Informe-2022-Articulo-19.pdf).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-07 — The Maya Train advanced but faced delays, cost escalation, and weak transparency

**Fact and significance.** The Maya Train, one of AMLO’s flagship projects, opened partially in December 2023 despite major delays, incomplete infrastructure, and substantial cost increases. The army built and operated part of the project. AMLO acknowledged that the project was not complete and could take years to cover operating costs.

**Strongest source.** Associated Press, *Mexico’s Maya tourist train opens for partial service amid delays and cost overruns*, 16 December 2023. [AP report](https://apnews.com/article/7b0db1b6b262acde6721debceaa5af7e). AP reported that the original projected cost was approximately $8.6 billion, while $22.7 billion had been assigned and the Treasury secretary estimated a final cost near $28 billion.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Direct. The project was a presidential priority, and the president publicly defended its progress.

**Complications.** The train began carrying passengers and was presented by the government as a regional development and tourism project. Supporters viewed its construction speed and expected social benefits positively.

**Cautions.** AP’s cost figures are based on government statements and public reporting; final cost requires audit completion. The project’s long-term benefits cannot be judged from its partial opening.

**Independent support.** Mexico’s Superior Audit Office produced 2023 audits of Maya Train sections, including contract and expenditure reviews. [ASF audit example](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0143_a.pdf).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-08 — Social transfers and macroeconomic continuity coexisted with service weaknesses

**Fact and significance.** AMLO expanded direct social transfers and emphasized welfare programs as the practical core of the Fourth Transformation. At the same time, BTI reported austerity, uneven public-service capacity, medicine shortages, and the loss of coverage associated with replacing Seguro Popular with INSABI. The government’s macroeconomic framework remained more orthodox than its anti-neoliberal rhetoric suggested.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 2–4, 7. [PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf).

**Period/status.** Mostly inherited policy direction continuing into 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Strong. Budget priorities, transfer design, health-system centralization, and austerity were executive policies.

**Complications.** The government reported increased welfare coverage and argued that direct transfers reduced corruption and intermediaries. Local data indicate substantial social-program reach, but coverage does not by itself establish improved outcomes.

**Cautions.** BTI’s account combines country-level statistics and expert judgments; the specific medicine-shortage and coverage claims require verification through health audits and household surveys.

**Independent support.** The presidential budget release describes increased funding for welfare programs. [2023 budget-policy release](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/paquete-economico-2023-prioriza-aumento-al-presupuesto-de-los-programas-para-el-bienestar-y-garantiza-financiamiento-de-obras).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-09 — Corruption claims coexisted with accountability and conflict-of-interest concerns

**Fact and significance.** AMLO made anti-corruption a central legitimacy claim, but major contracts were increasingly assigned directly to the armed forces or state enterprises, while autonomous accountability institutions faced political pressure and resource constraints. This created a tension between the administration’s anti-corruption narrative and reduced transparency in large projects.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 2–3. [PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf). BTI reports that accountability institutions were impeded through funding reductions, takeovers, or attempted abolition and that major public-project contracts were often awarded without public competition.

**Period/status.** Ongoing condition, with direct relevance to 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Strong. Procurement design, institutional appointments, and public accountability were substantially controlled by the federal executive and congressional coalition.

**Complications.** Direct contracting can be defended as faster or more secure for strategic projects; allegations of corruption do not establish unlawful diversion without audit or judicial findings.

**Cautions.** BTI provides an expert assessment rather than a finding of criminal liability. Each alleged irregularity should be linked to a specific ASF audit, investigation, or court proceeding.

**Independent support.** ASF audits of Maya Train contracts provide project-level evidence requiring separate examination. [ASF audit](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0143_a.pdf).

---

### Record AMLO-2023-10 — Foreign policy emphasized nonintervention and limited military involvement abroad

**Fact and significance.** Mexico did not enter an interstate war during 2023. AMLO’s foreign-policy posture emphasized constitutional principles of nonintervention and peaceful dispute settlement. The government also used military aviation to assist Mexican citizens during the Israel-Gaza crisis.

**Strongest source.** Presidency of Mexico, *Política exterior se fundamenta en principios constitucionales; SRE y Fuerza Aérea Mexicana asisten a mexicanos en Israel*, October 2023. [Presidential release](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/politica-exterior-se-fundamenta-en-principios-constitucionales-presidente-sre-y-fuerza-aerea-mexicana-asisten-a-mexicanos-en-israel).

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler connection.** Direct for executive foreign-policy messaging and consular operations; limited by treaty obligations, U.S. economic interdependence, and Mexico’s nonintervention tradition.

**Complications.** Humanitarian assistance and neutrality reduced direct military risk. Mexico’s diplomatic positions could nevertheless be criticized as insufficiently forceful by different domestic and international constituencies.

**Cautions.** The presidential release is self-presentational and does not independently assess diplomatic effectiveness.

**Independent support.** Researchers should compare Mexico’s official statements with UN voting records, State Department reporting, and contemporary Mexican diplomatic coverage.

## 3. Information environment and limitations

Information was available through official reports, court records, audit documents, international organizations, and investigative journalism, but reliability was uneven.

- Government communications were abundant, especially through daily presidential conferences, but were advocacy documents and often emphasized achievements.
- Crime and disappearance statistics were vulnerable to underreporting, changing definitions, registry revisions, and weak local investigative capacity.
- Criminal intimidation restricted reporting and civic activity in areas where cartels influenced local officials or controlled territory.
- Journalists and human-rights defenders faced violence and high impunity; this creates systematic under-observation, especially outside major cities.
- Political polarization made claims about electoral institutions, corruption, security, and infrastructure highly contested.
- Military involvement reduced transparency because security and infrastructure projects were frequently treated as matters of national security.
- Courts and the ASF provided important checks, but litigation and audits often lagged behind policy implementation.
- International comparative assessments such as BTI are useful for synthesis but should be decomposed into verifiable claims.

## 4. Questions for deeper research

1. Did homicide reductions in 2023 reflect genuine improved security, reporting changes, geographic concentration, or changes in criminal-group behavior?
2. How did the 2023 disappearance-registry revision alter the official total, and how many cases were independently verified?
3. What specific constitutional and administrative effects would the electoral Plan B have had before the Supreme Court invalidated it?
4. What was the final 2023 expenditure and committed cost of the Maya Train, and what did ASF audits establish as recoverable or irregular?
5. How much operational autonomy did the National Guard retain from SEDENA in practice?
6. Which corruption allegations involving relatives, officials, military contractors, and public projects produced formal investigations or convictions?
7. Did welfare transfers measurably improve poverty, health access, and living standards relative to comparable regions?
8. What role did criminal groups play in subnational elections and local political coercion?
9. How did AMLO’s public attacks on journalists affect specific outlets, investigations, or protection decisions?
10. What were the measurable foreign-policy outcomes of Mexico’s nonintervention posture in regional and global crises?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility — Andrés Manuel López Obrador, Mexico, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Mexico did not possess nuclear weapons in 2023 and was not involved in an interstate nuclear crisis. AMLO’s authority therefore concerned treaty diplomacy, nuclear and radiological regulation, safeguards, emergency preparedness, military and intelligence security, and broader catastrophic risks such as cyber operations.

The inherited posture was strongly non-nuclear. Mexico is party to the Treaty of Tlatelolco, the NPT safeguards system, the Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty, the Convention on Nuclear Safety, the Convention on the Physical Protection of Nuclear Material and its 2005 amendment, and the radioactive-waste safety convention. The IAEA lists Mexico’s safeguards arrangements and nuclear-safety treaties as in force. [IAEA Mexico country fact sheet](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=MX)

AMLO controlled foreign-policy direction and the federal executive, but treaty implementation was distributed among the Foreign Ministry, the National Commission for Nuclear Safety and Safeguards (CNSNS), the National Institute for Nuclear Research, the military, Congress, and international institutions. His government’s major catastrophic-risk weakness was not nuclear escalation but the expansion of military and intelligence power alongside demonstrated cyber-security and transparency problems.

The evidence is strongest for Mexico’s 2023 disarmament diplomacy and inherited treaty posture. It is weaker for annual nuclear-safety resources, regulator independence, and correction of cyber vulnerabilities because several Mexican government documents were inaccessible or unavailable through the supplied catalogue.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-1B-01 | Mexico’s inherited non-nuclear treaty and safeguards framework remained in force | 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.6, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-02 | Mexico presided over the 2023 TPNW meeting and promoted nuclear disarmament | 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-03 | Mexico participated in the 2023 CTBT entry-into-force process | 1B.1, 1B.4, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-04 | The federal government maintained a nuclear/radiological regulatory institution, but 2023 performance evidence remains incomplete | 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-05 | The Sedena cyber breach exposed serious information-security weaknesses and the administration’s response was disputed | 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.9 |
| WEB-1B-06 | AMLO defended military intelligence activity after Pegasus-related reporting, creating unresolved safeguards concerns | 1B.3, 1B.5, 1B.7, 1B.9 |

### WEB-1B-01 — Inherited non-nuclear treaty and safeguards framework

**Claim.** Mexico entered 2023 with a substantial legal framework excluding nuclear weapons and requiring safeguards, physical protection, accident notification, emergency assistance, nuclear safety, and radioactive-waste management.

**Why it matters.** This is the baseline against which any claim of a safer or less safe 2023 posture should be measured.

**Source.** *Mexico — Country Fact Sheet*, International Atomic Energy Agency, updated 2026 but reporting treaty status. [IAEA](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=MX)

**Locator.** “Agreements” and “Safeguards Agreements” sections: Convention on the Physical Protection of Nuclear Material and 2005 Amendment; Early Notification and Assistance Conventions; Convention on Nuclear Safety; Joint Convention; NPT/Tlatelolco safeguards; Additional Protocol.

**Period/status.** Inherited baseline, still applicable in 2023.

**Attribution.** Low-to-moderate for AMLO personally; the treaty obligations predate him. Moderate for the administration’s duty to preserve and implement them.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** Treaty membership does not prove effective implementation, funding, inspection quality, or emergency readiness. The page is current and not a 2023 snapshot.

**Independent corroboration.** IAEA national-report materials and CNSNS treaty pages were identified but not successfully opened.

### WEB-1B-02 — Mexico presided over the 2023 Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons meeting

**Claim.** Mexico presided over the Second Meeting of States Parties to the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons, held at UN headquarters from 27 November to 1 December 2023. Mexico’s ambassador Juan Ramón de la Fuente served as president, and Mexico’s Foreign Ministry described the meeting as a successful effort to advance the treaty’s global prohibition of nuclear weapons.

**Why it matters.** This is the clearest 2023 evidence of Mexico using diplomatic authority to reduce nuclear danger.

**Sources.**

- *Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons — Second Meeting of States Parties (2023)*, United Nations, 2023. [UNODA](https://meetings.unoda.org/tpnw/tpnw-msp-2023)
- *Concluye con éxito la segunda reunión de las partes del Tratado sobre la Prohibición de Armas Nucleares, presidida por México*, Secretaría de Relaciones Exteriores, 2 December 2023. [SRE](https://www.gob.mx/sre/prensa/concluye-con-exito-la-segunda-reunion-de-las-partes-del-tratado-sobre-la-prohibicion-de-armas-nucleares-presidida-por-mexico)

**Locator.** UNODA event description identifies dates and Mexico’s presidency; SRE communiqué describes the meeting’s outcome and the TPNW’s comprehensive prohibition.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong to the Mexican state and Foreign Ministry; moderate to AMLO personally because the activity was conducted through his administration, though the ambassador and diplomatic service had operational autonomy.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** The SRE source is self-presentational. Presidency of a treaty meeting is evidence of diplomatic engagement, not proof that nuclear-armed states changed policy.

**Independent corroboration.** ICAN independently records Mexico’s presidency of the meeting. [ICAN](https://www.icanw.org/tpnw_second_meeting_of_states_parties)

### WEB-1B-03 — Mexico participated in the CTBT entry-into-force process

**Claim.** Mexico was represented at the 22 September 2023 CTBTO Article XIV Conference, a formal process dedicated to bringing the Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty into force.

**Why it matters.** Participation supports a continuing Mexican policy of treaty-based nuclear restraint and verification.

**Source.** *2023 Conference on Facilitating the Entry into Force of the Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty*, CTBTO, 22 September 2023. [CTBTO](https://www.ctbto.org/our-mission/article-xiv-conferences/2023-conference-facilitating-entry-force-comprehensive-nuclear)

**Locator.** Conference description at lines 8–13; speaker list identifies Mexico’s Vice Minister for Multilateral Affairs and Human Rights, Joel Hernández García, at lines 89–90; conference materials at lines 199–225.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong to Mexico’s diplomatic apparatus; moderate to AMLO because the Foreign Ministry acted under his administration.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** Attendance and representation do not establish a new national commitment or measurable progress toward treaty entry into force.

**Independent corroboration.** CTBTO’s official event archive is itself the primary institutional record; a Mexican statement should be obtained for the exact position delivered.

### WEB-1B-04 — Nuclear and radiological regulation existed, but 2023 implementation is incompletely documented

**Claim.** Mexico maintained a specialized regulator, the CNSNS, responsible for nuclear safety and safeguards, and participated in IAEA nuclear-safety and technical-cooperation frameworks. However, the supplied 2023 CNSNS performance report and related regulatory materials could not be opened, so annual staffing, budget, inspections, incidents, enforcement, and corrective action remain unverified here.

**Why it matters.** Nuclear safety depends on competent, independent, adequately resourced implementation; treaty status alone is insufficient.

**Sources inspected or attempted.**

- *Informe sobre el Desempeño Anual 2023*, CNSNS. [Candidate PDF](https://www.cnsns.gob.mx/transparencia/archivos/informe_desempeno_anual_2023.pdf)
- *Qué hacemos*, CNSNS. [CNSNS](https://www.gob.mx/cnsns/que-hacemos)
- *Convención sobre Seguridad Nuclear*, CNSNS. [CNSNS](https://www.gob.mx/cnsns/documentos/convencion-sobre-seguridad-nuclear)
- *United Mexican States — National Report*, IAEA. [IAEA PDF](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/mexico_cns_national_report_2022.pdf)

**Period/status.** Target-year evidence sought; inherited institutional framework partly confirmed.

**Attribution.** Moderate. The regulator operated within AMLO’s executive structure, but operational safety decisions may have been made by CNSNS professionals.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** The absence of an accessible annual report is an information gap, not evidence of regulatory failure. The IAEA country page confirms treaty status but not 2023 performance.

### WEB-1B-05 — Sedena cyber breach exposed catastrophic-information-security weaknesses

**Claim.** The Guacamaya breach of Mexico’s Defense Ministry exposed millions of internal records and prompted concerns about military cyber-security, data protection, and institutional transparency. In 2023 the government’s response was criticized for minimizing or denying parts of the leaked material; separate reporting indicated that Sedena acknowledged recurring attacks and significant infrastructure weaknesses.

**Why it matters.** Cyber compromise of a powerful military institution can affect crisis stability, intelligence integrity, public safety, and protection of sensitive information. It is relevant to catastrophic dual-use risk even though it was not a nuclear incident.

**Sources.**

- *Guacamaya Leaks and the Ayotzinapa Case*, National Security Archive, 10 March 2023. [National Security Archive](https://nsarchive.gwu.edu/briefing-book/mexico-ayotzinapa/2023-03-10/guacamaya-leaks-and-ayotzinapa-case)
- *Titular de la Sedena rinde cuentas ante diputados por Guacamaya Leaks*, El Universal, 2023. [El Universal](https://www.eluniversal.com.mx/nacion/titular-de-la-sedena-rinde-cuentas-ante-diputados-por-guacamaya-leaks/)
- AMLO press conference transcript, 23 March 2023. [Presidencia](https://www.gob.mx/amlo/articulos/version-estenografica-conferencia-de-prensa-del-presidente-andres-manuel-lopez-obrador-del-23-de-marzo-de-2023)

**Locator.** National Security Archive describes the scale of the leak and government minimization; El Universal reports Sedena’s admission of recurrent attacks; the presidential transcript contains the administration’s response.

**Period/status.** The breach occurred in 2022; governmental response and accountability continued into 2023.

**Attribution.** Strong for the administration’s response and military cyber-governance; moderate for the original vulnerability because the breach predated the target year.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** The National Security Archive is an independent archival and research organization but relies partly on leaked material. Leaked documents require authentication and contextual verification. Government statements dispute or minimize some interpretations.

**Independent corroboration.** Contemporary reporting by Mexican outlets, cybersecurity specialists, and legislative testimony should be compared record by record.

### WEB-1B-06 — Intelligence safeguards and Pegasus controversy

**Claim.** In March 2023 AMLO defended military use of Pegasus-related intelligence as “investigation” rather than unlawful espionage, after reporting based on Guacamaya documents and Citizen Lab analysis linked military surveillance to journalists and activists investigating military abuses. The episode raised unresolved questions about judicial authorization, civilian oversight, protection of dissent, and the possibility that intelligence resources were used against accountability actors.

**Why it matters.** Even without nuclear weapons, intelligence safeguards are relevant to catastrophic-risk responsibility because unchecked security institutions can conceal failures, suppress expert warnings, and weaken crisis accountability.

**Source.** *López Obrador dice que el Ejército no espió con Pegasus a periodistas y activistas, sino que se hizo “investigación”*, El País México, 10 March 2023. [El País](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-03-10/lopez-obrador-dice-que-el-ejercito-no-espio-con-pegasus-a-periodistas-y-activistas.html)

**Locator.** Reported presidential statement; discussion of Sedena’s alleged Pegasus purchase and Citizen Lab’s technical findings concerning Raymundo Ramos’s device.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence, with alleged surveillance beginning earlier.

**Attribution.** Strong for AMLO’s public defense and administration’s position; moderate for the underlying surveillance because attribution depends on leaked documents and technical investigations.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** The article is high-quality reporting but not a final judicial finding. AMLO and Sedena denied that the activity constituted illegal espionage and characterized it as intelligence work.

**Independent corroboration.** R3D, Citizen Lab, ARTICLE 19, and the National Security Archive should be used to validate technical and legal details separately.

## 3. Disposition by lens

- **1B.1 — Reducing existential risk:** Supported. Mexico used diplomatic authority in the TPNW and CTBT processes. No evidence was found of an AMLO decision to increase nuclear escalation risk.
- **1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Partly supported. Mexico’s public diplomatic posture was disarmament-oriented. Direct 2023 AMLO nuclear statements were not located.
- **1B.3 — Safe nuclear control:** Open question. Treaty and safeguards structures existed, but 2023 regulator resources, inspections, incident data, and command arrangements remain insufficiently documented.
- **1B.4 — Arms control and inspections:** Supported for continuity and diplomacy. Mexico maintained safeguards and participated in TPNW and CTBT processes. Specific 2023 inspection performance remains a gap.
- **1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression:** No adverse nuclear evidence located. Broader military-intelligence opacity is relevant context but should not be converted into a nuclear finding.
- **1B.6 — Preventing proliferation:** Baseline supported by safeguards and regional non-nuclear commitments; 2023 enforcement and export-control performance remain unverified.
- **1B.7 — Independent risk expertise:** Mixed. Specialized nuclear institutions existed, but military intelligence opacity and disputed surveillance raise concerns about protected dissent and institutional independence.
- **1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation:** No nuclear crisis occurred in 2023. Mexico’s diplomatic posture was de-escalatory; direct crisis-management evidence is absent.
- **1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks:** Adverse evidence exists for cyber-security weakness and disputed intelligence safeguards. AI and biological-risk policy evidence was not located.
- **1B.10 — Lasting risk posture:** Mixed and unresolved. Mexico’s treaty-based nuclear posture remained stable and diplomatically active, but the durability and quality of nuclear regulation and broader cyber-security safeguards cannot yet be demonstrated.

## 4. Source groups

### Accepted evidence

1. IAEA, *Mexico Country Fact Sheet*.
2. UNODA, *Second Meeting of States Parties to the TPNW*.
3. SRE, Mexico’s 2023 TPNW presidency communiqué.
4. CTBTO, 2023 Article XIV Conference archive.
5. National Security Archive, Guacamaya Leaks and Ayotzinapa.
6. El Universal, Sedena testimony on Guacamaya.
7. Presidencia, 23 March 2023 press-conference transcript.
8. El País México, Pegasus and military-intelligence reporting.
9. ICAN, TPNW Second Meeting summary.

### Opened or useful mainly for corroboration

- BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico.
- UNODA Yearbook 2023.
- IAEA Joint Convention national-report material.
- SADER, SENER, SEMARNAT, and Secretaría de Salud sectoral programs.

These sources provide context on state capacity and policy but did not add a sufficiently specific nuclear-risk fact to the accepted record above.

### Access-blocked or not fully inspected

- CNSNS, *Informe sobre el Desempeño Anual 2023*.
- CNSNS pages on its mandate and Convention on Nuclear Safety.
- IAEA, Mexico National Report under the Convention on Nuclear Safety.
- Mexican SRE page on TPNW presidency, where direct opening returned HTTP 403.
- UNODA TPNW meeting page initially blocked JavaScript, though search results exposed its key metadata.
- SRE article, “Hacia la prohibición de las armas nucleares.”
- UN Member State statement on nuclear disarmament.
- CNSNS 2023 annual report and regulator-performance materials.
- IAEA regional radioactive-source emergency-management report.
- CTBTO conference report and Mexico’s exact statement.
- Mexican official cyber-intelligence and national-security releases.

## 5. Source counts and remaining gaps

At least 15 catalogue candidates were attempted. Nine supplied usable evidence or corroboration; six or more were blocked, timed out, or only partially accessible.

The main unresolved gaps are:

1. CNSNS 2023 budget, staffing, inspections, incidents, enforcement, and corrective actions.
2. Mexico’s exact 2023 statements and voting/negotiating positions at the TPNW and CTBT meetings.
3. 2023 IAEA safeguards conclusions and any compliance findings.
4. Export-control and proliferation-prevention enforcement.
5. Cyber-security budget and remediation after Guacamaya.
6. Whether military intelligence surveillance received judicial authorization and whether any officials were held accountable.
7. Any 2023 policy addressing AI, biological, or other catastrophic dual-use risks.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security — Andrés Manuel López Obrador, Mexico, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Mexico had no interstate war, territorial annexation campaign, or cross-border military operation in 2023. The relevant exposure gate is therefore negative for conventional international war but positive for:

- organized-crime violence with cross-border effects;
- U.S.-Mexico counternarcotics and migration pressure;
- military involvement in domestic security;
- diplomatic participation in Colombia’s peace process;
- overseas consular evacuations during the Israel-Gaza crisis; and
- unresolved accountability issues involving the Mexican armed forces.

AMLO formally controlled foreign policy, the armed forces, the National Guard, and federal security policy. His authority was limited by Congress, courts, state governments, the armed forces’ institutional autonomy, criminal organizations, Mexico’s treaty obligations, and dependence on U.S. cooperation.

The strongest favorable evidence is Mexico’s continued preference for dialogue, nonintervention, and multilateral peace diplomacy. The strongest adverse evidence concerns military expansion, reduced security cooperation with the United States, unresolved military accountability in the Ayotzinapa case, and disputed transparency by SEDENA. These latter issues concern lawful security and international accountability more than interstate conflict itself.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-01 | Mexico had no interstate war or external military campaign in 2023 and emphasized peaceful dispute resolution | 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-02 | Mexico participated as guarantor in Colombia’s peace dialogue with the ELN | 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-03 | Mexico’s security relationship with the United States weakened while counternarcotics pressures intensified | 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.8, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-04 | AMLO expanded military roles in domestic security and administration | 2B.2, 2B.4, 2B.8, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-05 | The GIEI documented military knowledge, intelligence activity, and obstruction in the Ayotzinapa investigation | 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-06 | The government allowed some independent investigation of Ayotzinapa but failed to secure full military cooperation | 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-07 | Mexico’s human-rights record and military accountability remained contested | 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-08 | Mexico used military aircraft to assist citizens during the Israel crisis without entering the conflict | 2B.1, 2B.2, 2B.3, 2B.10 |

### WEB-2B-01 — No interstate war and continued peaceful-dispute rhetoric

**Claim.** Mexico did not enter an interstate war or undertake a cross-border military campaign in 2023. In Mexico’s statement to the UN General Assembly, Foreign Secretary Alicia Bárcena reaffirmed Mexico’s commitment to dialogue, mutual understanding, peace, and peaceful resolution of disputes.

**Why it matters.** This establishes the conflict-exposure baseline and indicates that AMLO’s government did not initiate or prolong a conventional international war.

**Source.** *Statement by Mexico’s Secretary of Foreign Affairs during the General Debate of the 78th UN General Assembly*, Permanent Mission of Mexico to the UN, September 2023. [PDF](https://mision.sre.gob.mx/onu/images/2023/CSONU/Intervenciones/23Sep23_-__Statement_of_the_Secretary_of_Foreign_Affars_during_the_General_Debate_UNGA_78.pdf)

**Locator.** Statement at pp. 4–5: Mexico reaffirmed dialogue, mutual understanding, peaceful resolution of disputes, and participation in regional peace efforts.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong for the Mexican government’s diplomatic posture; moderate for AMLO personally because the statement was delivered by the Foreign Ministry.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** Official rhetoric does not prove peaceful conduct in domestic security operations or demonstrate that Mexico’s diplomatic efforts produced settlements.

### WEB-2B-02 — Mexico’s role in Colombia’s peace dialogue

**Claim.** Mexico participated as a guarantor country in the peace dialogue between the Colombian government and the National Liberation Army (ELN).

**Why it matters.** This is a concrete peaceful-security activity rather than merely a rhetorical commitment.

**Source.** *Statement by Mexico’s Secretary of Foreign Affairs during the General Debate of the 78th UN General Assembly*, Permanent Mission of Mexico to the UN, September 2023. [PDF](https://mision.sre.gob.mx/onu/images/2023/CSONU/Intervenciones/23Sep23_-__Statement_of_the_Secretary_of_the_Foreign_Affars_during_the_General_Debate_UNGA_78.pdf)

**Locator.** Page 4: Mexico is identified as a guarantor country in the “Peace Dialogue Table” between Colombia and the ELN.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence; the peace process predates 2023.

**Attribution.** Strong to Mexico’s Foreign Ministry and administration; moderate to AMLO personally.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** Guarantor participation does not establish that Mexico controlled the parties or achieved a durable settlement.

**Independent corroboration.** The Colombian peace-process record and UN reporting should be consulted for Mexico’s precise role and performance.

### WEB-2B-03 — Weakened U.S.-Mexico security cooperation

**Claim.** U.S.-Mexico security cooperation diminished under AMLO compared with earlier administrations. The Congressional Research Service reported that Mexico paused some DEA visa issuance, slowed extraditions, and declared the Mérida Initiative dead, even as fentanyl trafficking and arms trafficking increased pressure for cooperation.

**Why it matters.** The policy involved peaceful alternatives, international security coordination, and the use of military and law-enforcement resources. It also created external constraints on Mexico’s ability to manage transnational crime without escalation.

**Source.** *Mexico: Background and U.S. Relations*, Congressional Research Service, 10 May 2023. [PDF](https://www.everycrsreport.com/files/2023-05-10_R42917_2a484c222f89653e6ec88cdbcc7e91d664342f6c.pdf)

**Locator.** Pages 16–18: discussion of reduced cooperation since 2018, paused DEA visas, slower extraditions, the end of Mérida, the Bicentennial Framework, and fentanyl-control pressure.

**Period/status.** Broader AMLO-period condition continuing into 2023.

**Attribution.** Strong to AMLO’s administration; policy decisions concerned the executive and Foreign Ministry, though bilateral outcomes also depended on U.S. actions.

**Complications.** Mexico continued the Bicentennial Framework and retained security cooperation with the United States. Reduced cooperation may have reflected sovereignty concerns and opposition to earlier U.S. approaches rather than a desire to enable criminal violence.

**Cautions.** CRS is a U.S. legislative research product and summarizes contested bilateral issues; it is not a neutral causal evaluation.

### WEB-2B-04 — Expansion of military roles in domestic security

**Claim.** AMLO expanded the military’s role in public security and administrative tasks, despite campaigning against the military-led “war” on drugs. The CSIS assessment described expanded military influence, use of the National Guard, and reduced cooperation with the United States on security and counternarcotics issues.

**Why it matters.** Although primarily domestic, the policy affected lawful security, military-resource use, cross-border cooperation, and the durability of civilian control.

**Source.** *After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024*, Center for Strategic and International Studies, July 2023. [PDF](https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf?VersionId=3UYsTTmLo1Zf_Q8Cjb1ue.TwD8kTagkZ)

**Locator.** Pages 4–5 and 16–18: AMLO’s stated demilitarized approach contrasted with expanded military influence; pages 24–25 discuss militarization and civilian-security implications.

**Period/status.** Ongoing policy during 2023; some legal measures originated earlier.

**Attribution.** Strong. Military expansion was a central federal executive policy.

**Complications.** The administration argued that the armed forces were needed to confront criminal organizations and provide security where civilian institutions were weak. The Supreme Court blocked the transfer of the National Guard fully under military control, demonstrating an institutional check.

**Cautions.** CSIS is a policy institute with a critical assessment of AMLO; claims about causation between militarization and violence require separate statistical analysis.

### WEB-2B-05 — GIEI findings on military knowledge and failures in Ayotzinapa

**Claim.** The Independent Interdisciplinary Group of Experts (GIEI) reported in 2023 that military and other authorities had real-time information about the 2014 attacks on Ayotzinapa students, that military intelligence had information about the victims’ possible fate, and that no effective rescue or protection action followed.

**Why it matters.** This is a major accountability and lawful-security record involving the armed forces. It is not an international war finding, but it bears directly on whether the administration accepted independent scrutiny and remedied security-force harm.

**Source.** *Informe GIEI V — Una visión global sobre los hechos, las responsabilidades y la situación del caso Ayotzinapa*, GIEI, 31 March 2023. [PDF](https://hchr.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/01.-Informe-Ayotzinapa-V-2023.pdf)

**Locator.** Pages 5–11, especially findings 1–14. The report states that authorities knew of the attacks in real time, military intelligence had information, and no protective intervention occurred.

**Period/status.** Later retrospective evidence concerning 2014, produced during the target year.

**Attribution.** Strong for institutional conduct and the AMLO administration’s investigation context; weak for personal responsibility for the original 2014 events.

**Complications.** The GIEI continued working with Mexican authorities and families, and AMLO ordered access to some military archives. The report therefore records both cooperation and obstruction.

**Cautions.** The GIEI is an independent expert mechanism, but its conclusions concern an unresolved investigation and include allegations requiring judicial determination.

### WEB-2B-06 — Partial cooperation and continuing military obstruction

**Claim.** The GIEI’s 2023 follow-up report found that SEDENA repeatedly denied possessing documents that the GIEI and the presidential commission had located in military archives, including records concerning interceptions and military intelligence. The report characterized these denials as obstructing the investigation.

**Why it matters.** It provides direct evidence about accountability, transparency, and whether military institutions complied with civilian investigative demands.

**Source.** *Informe Ayotzinapa VI — Hallazgos, avances, obstáculos y pendientes*, GIEI, 2023. [PDF](https://hchr.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/2023_Informe_Ayotzinapa_VI.pdf)

**Locator.** Pages 195–218, especially pp. 199–200 and 214–218. The report lists repeated SEDENA denials concerning the CRFI intelligence center, intercepts, documents, and the chain of command.

**Period/status.** Target-year retrospective evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong for SEDENA’s conduct during the AMLO administration. Personal attribution to AMLO is mixed: the report states that he ordered access to archives, but SEDENA continued to deny or withhold material.

**Complications.** The government established the presidential truth commission and permitted the GIEI to continue its work. These are meaningful corrective actions even though cooperation remained incomplete.

**Cautions.** The GIEI report is not a court judgment. Its conclusions rely on documentary and testimonial evidence, some of which remains contested.

### WEB-2B-07 — Human-rights and military-accountability concerns

**Claim.** Amnesty International reported that military personnel were linked to enforced disappearance, extrajudicial executions, torture, and other abuses, and called for stronger civilian accountability and a plan to withdraw armed forces from public-security and administrative tasks.

**Why it matters.** Civilian protection and remedy are central to lawful security, even absent an international armed conflict.

**Source.** *The State of the World’s Human Rights: Mexico 2023*, Amnesty International, 2023. [PDF](https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/AMR4169922023ENGLISH.pdf)

**Locator.** Pages 2, 4–5: Supreme Court and National Guard controversy; military links to Ayotzinapa, extrajudicial executions and torture; recommendations for civilian accountability.

**Period/status.** 2023 assessment of inherited and continuing conditions.

**Attribution.** Moderate-to-strong for the administration’s continuation of military public-security policy and its response to judicial constraints.

**Complications.** The Supreme Court blocked the attempt to place the National Guard under military control, and the government did establish investigation mechanisms for past abuses.

**Cautions.** Amnesty is an advocacy organization. Its findings should be paired with court records, official investigations, and the GIEI documentation.

### WEB-2B-08 — Overseas assistance without military entry into a foreign conflict

**Claim.** During the Israel crisis in October 2023, Mexico used diplomatic and military-aviation resources to assist Mexican citizens while maintaining a stated foreign-policy commitment to constitutional principles and nonintervention. No evidence located indicates that Mexico joined the conflict or provided combat support.

**Why it matters.** This is a limited but concrete case of crisis response without escalation.

**Source.** Presidential release, *Política exterior se fundamenta en principios constitucionales; SRE y Fuerza Aérea Mexicana asisten a mexicanos en Israel*, October 2023. [Presidency of Mexico](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/politica-exterior-se-fundamenta-en-principios-constitucionales-presidente-sre-y-fuerza-aerea-mexicana-asisten-a-mexicanos-en-israel)

**Locator.** Government statement describing Mexico’s foreign-policy principles and assistance to Mexicans in Israel.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong for executive operational conduct.

**Cautions and contrary evidence.** The source is official and self-presentational. It establishes the evacuation/assistance action, not its completeness, cost, or diplomatic effect.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **2B.1 — Peaceful alternatives:** Supported. Mexico emphasized dialogue, peaceful dispute resolution, Colombia’s peace process, and continued diplomatic engagement. The absence of a conventional war reduced the need for force-based decisions.
- **2B.10 — Lasting international peace:** Partly supported. Mexico’s diplomatic posture and peace-process participation were stabilizing, but reduced U.S. security cooperation and military institutional expansion complicate the longer-term picture.
- **2B.2 — Starting or prolonging war:** No evidence of Mexico initiating or prolonging an interstate war, annexation campaign, or proxy war in 2023. Domestic militarization should not be treated as international war evidence.
- **2B.3 — Honest justification for force:** Mixed and underdeveloped. No international war justification was located. Security rhetoric should be compared with independent evidence on cartel violence, fentanyl, extraditions, and military necessity.
- **2B.4 — Civilian and prisoner protection:** Adverse evidence exists from Ayotzinapa and broader military-abuse findings, though these concern inherited events and domestic operations rather than a 2023 international conflict.
- **2B.5 — Control of proxies and arms:** No material 2023 evidence located concerning Mexican support for foreign proxies or arms recipients. U.S.-Mexico arms-trafficking and counternarcotics cooperation require further review.
- **2B.6 — Scrutiny of war claims:** Mixed. The GIEI and presidential commission were permitted to operate, but SEDENA’s document denials obstructed scrutiny.
- **2B.7 — Ceasefires and settlements:** Supported in a limited sense through Mexico’s guarantor role in Colombia’s ELN process. No Mexico-specific settlement was concluded.
- **2B.8 — Responsible military resources:** Mixed-to-adverse. Military resources were expanded into domestic security and civilian administration; transparency and proportionality remain contested.
- **2B.9 — Accountability and remedy:** Mixed-to-adverse. AMLO authorized some archive access and supported investigative institutions, but military noncooperation and unresolved abuses limited remedy.

## 4. Information environment

Reliable information was available from UN records, CRS, GIEI investigations, Amnesty, CSIS, and official Mexican statements. The main limitations were:

- military secrecy and repeated denials of records;
- contested evidence from intelligence archives and protected witnesses;
- disagreement between official security narratives and independent investigations;
- domestic-security facts being difficult to separate from international-security consequences;
- incomplete access to official operational and budget reports; and
- strong political polarization around the military, the United States, and the Ayotzinapa investigation.

## 5. Source groups

### Accepted evidence

1. Permanent Mission of Mexico to the UN, 78th UN General Assembly statement.

3. CSIS, *After AMLO*, July 2023.
4. GIEI, *Informe V*, March 2023.
5. GIEI, *Informe VI*, 2023.
6. Amnesty International, *Mexico 2023*.
7. Presidency of Mexico, Israel assistance statement.

- Presidency release on Israel assistance: cache miss during inspection.
- Fifth Government Report: cache miss.
- Centro Prodh report: HTTP 406.
- Crisis Group Mexico page: internal fetch error.
- Several gob.mx security and National Guard pages: fetch errors.
- Mexico’s EU-CELAC page: fetch error.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

At least 16 catalogue candidates were attempted. Eight produced accepted evidence; eight were opened mainly for corroboration or were blocked.

Remaining research gaps:

1. Exact 2023 military and National Guard budgets, procurement, and deployment data.
2. Whether U.S.-Mexico cooperation changes produced measurable effects on fentanyl, arms trafficking, or extraditions.
3. Mexican legal and operational rules governing military force and civilian protection.
4. Judicial findings concerning SEDENA obstruction and Ayotzinapa accountability.
5. Mexico’s precise role and outcomes in Colombia’s ELN peace process.
6. Full operational record of the Israel evacuation.
7. Independent evaluation of whether militarization improved or worsened security outcomes.
