--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection — Andrés Manuel López Obrador, Mexico, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador was Mexico’s president and controlled the federal executive, the National Guard, SEDENA, the National Migration Institute, the federal prosecutor’s office, and national search and human-rights policy. Policing and prosecution remained substantially decentralized across states and municipalities, while organized criminal groups exercised territorial influence in several regions. Attribution to AMLO is therefore strongest for federal policy, military conduct, migration detention, national-search administration, public rhetoric, and the federal response to documented abuse; state and municipal violence requires more limited attribution.

Mexico entered 2023 with very high levels of criminal violence, disappearances, impunity, military involvement in public security, attacks on journalists, and weak investigative capacity. The principal adverse 2023 evidence concerns:

- unlawful killings and alleged extrajudicial killings by SEDENA personnel;
- continuing disappearances and disputed registry administration;
- torture, arbitrary detention, and harsh detention conditions;
- the deaths of 40 migrants in an INM detention-center fire;
- military obstruction and incomplete cooperation in the Ayotzinapa investigation;
- intimidation of journalists, protesters, and human-rights defenders.

There is also contrary or mitigating evidence: homicide indicators reportedly declined from earlier peaks; federal authorities prosecuted some security personnel; the government supported the Ayotzinapa truth commission and search operations; independent monitoring remained possible; and the Supreme Court constrained military control of the National Guard.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-3B-01 | Persistent criminal violence, disappearances, and weak accountability | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-02 | Federal security forces were implicated in unlawful killings in Nuevo Laredo | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-03 | Disappearance data and investigations were incomplete and contested | 3B.1, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-04 | Ayotzinapa investigation documented military knowledge and obstruction | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-05 | Torture, arbitrary detention, and prison abuse persisted | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.5 |
| WEB-3B-06 | Ciudad Juárez migrant detention fire killed 40 people | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.7, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-07 | Journalists faced state and criminal intimidation and high impunity | 3B.3, 3B.4, 3B.8, 3B.10 |
| WEB-3B-08 | Protesters and defenders faced excessive force and retaliation | 3B.3, 3B.4, 3B.7, 3B.8, 3B.9 |
| WEB-3B-09 | The administration took some corrective and accountability measures | 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.9, 3B.10 |

### WEB-3B-01 — Persistent criminal violence and weak accountability

**Claim.** Criminal organizations remained major perpetrators of homicide, torture, kidnapping, extortion, trafficking, and intimidation in 2023. The U.S. State Department reported that most such crimes remained uninvestigated and unprosecuted, while disappearances, fragmented forensic systems, and weak data-sharing persisted.

**Why it matters.** This establishes the national physical-safety environment and the inherited conditions against which AMLO’s security strategy should be assessed.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State, 2024, reporting on 2023. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)

**Locator.** Executive summary, lines 7–10; disappearance section, lines 23–42.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 evidence and inherited structural condition.

**Attribution.** Moderate for AMLO. Criminal violence was not wholly under federal control, but federal security strategy, investigative capacity, and coordination were within the administration’s authority.

**Complications.** The government reported declining homicide levels and conducted searches, arrests, and prosecutions. State-level variation was substantial.

**Cautions.** The State Department report synthesizes government, NGO, media, and other sources; it does not provide a single causal estimate of presidential responsibility.

### WEB-3B-02 — SEDENA killings in Nuevo Laredo

**Claim.** In February 2023, SEDENA personnel in Nuevo Laredo shot at a vehicle, killing five civilians and wounding one. In a separate May incident, soldiers appeared in media footage to commit extrajudicial killings of five civilians. Military and civilian investigations followed; 16 soldiers, including a commanding officer, were confined in military prison in connection with the May case.

**Why it matters.** These are direct 2023 examples of lethal force by federal military personnel and subsequent accountability.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State, 2024. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)

**Locator.** Section 1.A, lines 13–22.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong institutional attribution to SEDENA; moderate-to-strong presidential attribution because SEDENA operated under federal executive command, although individual criminal liability remained individualized.

**Complications.** Investigations and confinement show that the government did not simply ignore every alleged abuse. The report also notes that some civilians were alleged cartel members and that soldiers claimed they were responding to gunfire.

**Cautions.** The State Department describes reports and investigations, not final judgments in every case. The May incident’s legal outcome requires follow-up.

### WEB-3B-03 — Disappearance registry and investigative weakness

**Claim.** The official registry listed 113,188 disappeared persons between 1964 and August 2023, but federal and state databases were incomplete, forensic systems fragmented, and government statistics often merged enforced disappearances with other missing-person cases. The registry had previously been removed from public access after AMLO classified the information as reserved.

**Why it matters.** Accurate data and accessible registries are prerequisites for protection, search, investigation, and remedy. Administrative uncertainty also made it difficult to measure whether people became safer.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State, 2024. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)

**Locator.** Section 1.B, lines 23–42.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 evidence with earlier administrative history.

**Attribution.** Strong for federal information policy and search administration; weaker for the underlying national disappearance total because state and local authorities controlled much of the data.

**Complications.** The government launched generalized search operations, reported exhumations and identifications, and continued the National Search Commission’s work.

**Cautions.** Registry totals and classification practices were politically contested. The report itself notes that victims of enforced disappearance and other missing persons were often combined.

### WEB-3B-04 — Ayotzinapa: military knowledge and obstruction

**Claim.** The GIEI’s 2023 reports found that military and other authorities had real-time information about the 2014 Ayotzinapa attacks and relevant communications, while SEDENA repeatedly denied possessing documents that investigators later found in military archives. The GIEI concluded that these denials obstructed the investigation.

**Why it matters.** This is the strongest evidence concerning federal security-force accountability, command transparency, and remedy for grave abuse.

**Sources.**

- *Informe GIEI V*, GIEI, 31 March 2023. [PDF](https://hchr.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/01.-Informe-Ayotzinapa-V-2023.pdf)
- *Informe Ayotzinapa VI*, GIEI, 2023. [PDF](https://hchr.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/2023_Informe_Ayotzinapa_VI.pdf)

**Locator.** GIEI V, pp. 5–11; GIEI VI, pp. 195–218, especially pp. 199–200 and 214–218.

**Period/status.** Later retrospective evidence published during 2023 concerning 2014–2023 investigation conduct.

**Attribution.** Strong to SEDENA’s conduct during AMLO’s administration. Personal attribution is mixed: AMLO ordered access to some archives and supported a truth commission, but the military continued to deny or withhold information.

**Complications.** The administration permitted the GIEI to continue its work, created the Presidential Truth and Justice Commission, and issued or reissued arrest warrants.

**Cautions.** The GIEI reports are independent investigative findings, not final criminal judgments. Some evidence came from protected witnesses and intelligence materials.

### WEB-3B-05 — Torture, arbitrary detention, and prison abuse

**Claim.** In 2023, federal and state security personnel—including the National Guard, armed forces, Attorney General’s Office, security authorities, and migration officials—were the subject of complaints concerning torture and arbitrary detention. Prisons and migrant detention centers were often overcrowded, unsafe, and lacking adequate medical and protective services.

**Why it matters.** This directly concerns protection from state abuse, lawful coercion, detention safeguards, and the physical safety of vulnerable detainees.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State, 2024. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)

**Locator.** Torture section, lines 43–50; prison conditions, lines 51–64; arbitrary detention, lines 65–78.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence and inherited pattern.

**Attribution.** Moderate-to-strong for federal institutions; lower for state and municipal police, where AMLO lacked direct command.

**Complications.** Federal law prohibited torture, independent monitoring by the CNDH and ICRC was permitted, and some investigations and prosecutions occurred.

**Cautions.** Complaint totals are not equivalent to proven violations. The report combines federal and subnational evidence.

### WEB-3B-06 — Ciudad Juárez migrant detention fire

**Claim.** On 27 March 2023, 40 migrants died and 27 were seriously injured in a fire at an INM detention facility in Ciudad Juárez. The State Department reported that guards failed to unlock the holding-cell doors, while the INM acknowledged the loss of keys and the deaths and injuries. Amnesty linked the disaster to restrictive detention practices and noted that the Supreme Court had ruled migrants should not be held beyond 36 hours.

**Why it matters.** This is a direct, high-severity failure to protect people held in federal custody and a clear test of crisis response, detention safety, and remedy.

**Sources.**

- *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)
- *Avanza atención a víctimas y recomendaciones tras sucesos en Ciudad Juárez*, Instituto Nacional de Migración. [INM](https://www.gob.mx/inm/prensa/avanza-atencion-a-victimas-y-recomendaciones-tras-sucesos-en-ciudad-juarez?idiom=es-MX)
- *Mexico: Fatal fire in migrant detention center is result of inhumane policies*, Amnesty International, 28 March 2023. [Amnesty](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/03/mexico-fatal-fire-immigration-detention-cinhumane-policies/)

**Locator.** State Department report, lines 53–64; INM acknowledgment of 40 deaths, 27 injuries, and lost keys; Amnesty discussion of detention limits and Supreme Court ruling.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong. The facility was administered by the federal INM, and the president and federal executive controlled migration policy and agency leadership.

**Complications.** Authorities opened criminal investigations, detained or charged officials, and announced victim assistance and reforms.

**Cautions.** The exact chain of individual responsibility and final judicial outcomes require separate court records. Amnesty’s structural-policy conclusion is an advocacy assessment.

### WEB-3B-07 — Journalists faced intimidation and impunity

**Claim.** Journalists covering crime, corruption, and local politics faced killings, disappearance, threats, physical attacks, cyberattacks, and self-censorship. The State Department reported that officials, criminal organizations, and public-advertising dependence all contributed to pressure; AMLO repeatedly publicly discredited journalists and civil-society organizations.

**Why it matters.** Journalism is an important protection mechanism for documenting abuse. Intimidation weakens reporting, complaint channels, and accountability.

**Sources.**

- *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)
- *Mexico Archives*, Committee to Protect Journalists, 2023. [CPJ](https://cpj.org/americas/mexico/2023/)

**Locator.** State Department report, lines 108–144; CPJ reports on Luis Martín Sánchez Íñiguez and AMLO’s attacks on media and ARTICLE 19.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence with inherited pattern.

**Attribution.** Moderate. Criminal groups caused many attacks, but federal protection, investigations, public rhetoric, and the presidential press environment were within AMLO’s authority.

**Complications.** Independent media remained active, internet access was not generally blocked, and the president repealed the 1917 Printing Crimes Law on 25 April 2023.

**Cautions.** Attribution for individual journalist killings is often uncertain. Public criticism is not itself physical violence, but can matter where threats and attacks follow.

### WEB-3B-08 — Protest, defenders, and excessive force

**Claim.** The State Department reported excessive force and arbitrary detention against protesters, including women protesting gender-based violence. It also reported that the National Guard removed and seized property from protesters in August 2023. The government criticized peaceful demonstrators protesting electoral-funding cuts, while human-rights defenders faced violence and intimidation.

**Why it matters.** This concerns the safety of dissent, protection of vulnerable groups, and whether federal security bodies used coercion narrowly.

**Source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State, 2024. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)

**Locator.** Assembly and association section, lines 145–149; environmental-defender killing, lines 19–21.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Moderate. The National Guard incident is directly federal; other protest policing was often state or municipal.

**Complications.** Constitutional assembly rights remained in force, and many protests proceeded without reported state violence. The president’s rhetoric did not always translate into coercive action.

**Cautions.** The report does not provide a complete event-level dataset or establish a nationwide presidential order to suppress protest.

### WEB-3B-09 — Corrective and accountability measures

**Claim.** The administration took some measures consistent with remedy: it created and maintained the Ayotzinapa truth and justice process, issued or reissued arrest warrants, conducted searches and exhumations, allowed monitoring by the CNDH and ICRC, and confined or investigated soldiers implicated in alleged unlawful killings.

**Why it matters.** These actions complicate an account based solely on tolerated abuse and show that federal response included genuine investigative and remedial elements.

**Sources.**

- *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*, U.S. Department of State. [Report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107784.html)
- *Informe GIEI V*, GIEI. [PDF](https://hchr.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/01.-Informe-Ayotzinapa-V-2023.pdf)
- *Informe Ayotzinapa VI*, GIEI. [PDF](https://hchr.mx/wp/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/2023_Informe_Ayotzinapa_VI.pdf)

**Locator.** State Department lines 16–18, 31–42, and 63–64; GIEI V pp. 22–27; GIEI VI pp. 195–218.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year implementation and retrospective accountability.

**Attribution.** Strong for federal actions; mixed for outcomes because prosecutions and convictions remained incomplete.

**Cautions.** Arrests, investigations, and commissions do not establish effective remedy. The GIEI documented continuing obstruction.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **3B.1 — Protection from state abuse:** Adverse evidence is substantial. 2023 included alleged unlawful killings, torture complaints, arbitrary detention, migrant deaths in federal custody, and continuing military accountability failures. Corrective investigations partly mitigate but do not resolve the record.
- **3B.10 — Lasting domestic safety:** Unresolved and mixed. Criminal violence and disappearances remained severe, while some homicide indicators declined and search/prosecution efforts continued. Durability cannot be established from 2023 alone.
- **3B.2 — Control of coercive forces:** Mixed-to-adverse. Federal forces were investigated in some cases, but military and migration-security abuses, institutional opacity, and incomplete discipline indicate weak control.
- **3B.3 — Incitement and targeted hatred:** Mixed-to-adverse. AMLO repeatedly stigmatized journalists, opponents, and protesters; evidence of direct presidential incitement to physical attacks is not established, but the rhetoric occurred in a threatening environment.
- **3B.4 — Complaints and independent oversight:** Mixed. CNDH, ICRC, GIEI, courts, and federal prosecutors operated, but military noncooperation, weak prosecution, and incomplete remedies limited effectiveness.
- **3B.5 — Emergency and surveillance powers:** Adverse evidence exists for military intelligence opacity, disputed surveillance, and arbitrary detention. A comprehensive 2023 legal review of surveillance authorizations remains a gap.
- **3B.6 — Safety without state terror:** Mixed-to-adverse. Criminal violence remained high and military expansion did not eliminate insecurity; available evidence does not establish a nationwide state-terror campaign.
- **3B.7 — Protection of vulnerable groups:** Adverse evidence includes migrant detention deaths, gender-based violence, attacks on defenders, and prison neglect affecting women, children, Indigenous people, disabled people, and LGBTQI+ detainees.
- **3B.8 — Safe protest and dissent:** Mixed-to-adverse. Peaceful assembly was legally protected, but some protests faced excessive force, property seizure, stigmatizing rhetoric, or weak investigation.
- **3B.9 — Restrained crisis response:** Adverse evidence is strongest for the Ciudad Juárez fire and the government’s initial blame-focused narrative; investigations and victim assistance are mitigating factors.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Information was unusually difficult to obtain and interpret because:

- disappearances were undercounted or inconsistently classified;
- local, state, and federal databases were fragmented;
- forensic capacity was insufficient for the caseload;
- criminal groups intimidated journalists, victims, and officials;
- military records were withheld or disputed;
- public officials criticized journalists and civil society;
- many allegations involved decentralized state or municipal police; and
- official figures often described activities and arrests but not final convictions, remedy, or recurrence.

The strongest sources were the U.S. State Department’s country report, GIEI investigations, the INM’s own acknowledgment of the Ciudad Juárez disaster, Amnesty, CPJ, and official statistical publications. Government claims of improved safety should be checked against INEGI, SESNSP, UCDP, ACLED, victim organizations, and local reporting.

## 5. Source groups

### Accepted evidence

1. U.S. Department of State, *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Mexico*.
2. GIEI, *Informe V*.
3. GIEI, *Informe Ayotzinapa VI*.
4. Instituto Nacional de Migración, Ciudad Juárez fire response.
5. Amnesty International, Ciudad Juárez fire assessment.
6. CPJ, Mexico 2023 journalist documentation.
7. INEGI, preliminary homicide statistics.
8. UN Working Group on Arbitrary Detention, Mexico visit.
9. ACLED Mexico country and conflict-monitoring material.

### Opened and useful mainly for corroboration

- Freedom House, *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023*.
- Amnesty International Mexico country report.
- Comisión para la Verdad y Acceso a la Justicia del Caso Ayotzinapa.
- CNDH disappearance materials.
- Global Witness Mexico defender documentation.
- Crisis Group Mexico program.
- Oxford academic work on AMLO and militarization.
- UNAM and other legal scholarship on the National Guard.
- official Fifth Government Report.

### Promising leads still requiring further inspection

- Full 2023 National Search Commission registry methodology and revision history.
- SESNSP monthly homicide, femicide, and gender-violence data.
- National Guard complaints, use-of-force records, and disciplinary files.
- INM detention-center inspections and final Ciudad Juárez case judgments.
- Federal and military court outcomes in the Nuevo Laredo killings.
- CNDH’s 2023 annual report and recommendations.
- CPJ and ARTICLE 19 case-level records for every journalist attack in 2023.
- Official state-level police and prosecutor records for high-violence states.

### Access-blocked or partially blocked

- AP reports on disappearance-registry changes.
- UN Working Group on Arbitrary Detention Mexico page.
- CPJ country archive direct page.
- CNDH disappearance report.
- ACLED conflict-watchlist and regional-overview pages.
- Crisis Group Mexico page.
- Several official Mexican portals.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

At least 16 candidates were inspected or attempted. Nine supplied accepted evidence or material corroboration; the remainder were opened for context or were blocked.

Key remaining gaps are:

1. Whether homicide reductions represented genuine exposure-adjusted safety gains.
2. Final convictions and disciplinary outcomes for federal-force abuses.
3. The complete causal and legal chain behind the Ciudad Juárez fire.
4. Whether AMLO’s public rhetoric measurably increased attacks against journalists or protesters.
5. Federal funding and staffing for search, forensic, victim-protection, and torture-prevention institutions.
6. The practical independence of the CNDH, prosecutors, and military courts.
7. Comparative state-level evidence distinguishing federal policy from local policing failure.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Political Freedom vs. Authoritarian Rule — Andrés Manuel López Obrador, Mexico, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Mexico remained an electoral democracy in 2023, with competitive parties, opposition-controlled regions, an independent judiciary with meaningful capacity, a constitutional one-term presidency, and no evidence that AMLO attempted to cancel elections or extend his own term. Freedom House classified Mexico as “Partly Free,” noting routine alternation in power and competitive opposition parties.

At the same time, AMLO’s administration pursued reforms and political practices that could weaken democratic checks: the electoral “Plan B,” pressure on the INE and TEPJF, attacks on the Supreme Court and journalists, weakening of autonomous oversight institutions, military expansion, and disputed spyware activity. The Supreme Court’s invalidation of Plan B demonstrated that institutional checks still operated.

AMLO’s practical power was constrained by Congress’s lack of a constitutional supermajority, the courts, autonomous electoral authorities, opposition parties, state governments, organized crime, and civil society. The principal 2023 question is therefore not whether Mexico became a closed authoritarian regime, but whether the administration weakened the institutional conditions that made future alternation and accountability possible.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-4B-01 | Mexico retained competitive elections, opposition parties, term limits, and realistic alternation | 4B.1, 4B.8, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-02 | Plan B sought to reduce electoral oversight capacity | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-03 | The Supreme Court invalidated Plan B on procedural grounds | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-04 | AMLO publicly pressured the INE, TEPJF, Supreme Court, and autonomous institutions | 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.4, 4B.5 |
| WEB-4B-05 | Journalists and critical media faced intimidation, public attacks, and violence | 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9 |
| WEB-4B-06 | Spyware and digital-surveillance allegations affected journalists, activists, and officials | 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9 |
| WEB-4B-07 | Internet connectivity remained broadly open, but propaganda and removal-request opacity persisted | 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9 |
| WEB-4B-08 | Women and Indigenous groups gained representation but remained underrepresented or exposed to unequal access | 4B.7 |
| WEB-4B-09 | Military expansion and criminal influence weakened democratic accountability in some regions | 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.10 |

### WEB-4B-01 — Competitive elections and realistic alternation remained

**Claim.** Mexico retained a multiparty electoral system, routine alternation in national and subnational power, a constitutional six-year presidential term without reelection, and realistic opposition opportunities. MORENA and its allies held a congressional majority after 2021 but lacked the supermajority needed for constitutional amendments.

**Why it matters.** This is the principal evidence against describing Mexico in 2023 as a fully authoritarian system or claiming that AMLO had already eliminated electoral competition.

**Source.** *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** Overview, lines 29–30; electoral system and term limits, lines 40–50; political pluralism and opposition opportunity, lines 65–74.

**Period/status.** Ruler-period and inherited institutional condition; much of the source assesses 2022, the baseline entering 2023.

**Attribution.** Low-to-moderate personal attribution. These were inherited constitutional and electoral institutions, though AMLO’s administration operated within and attempted to modify them.

**Complications.** Freedom House also reported criminal interference, misuse of public resources, and repeated attacks on INE and TEPJF.

**Cautions.** The report’s main developments concern 2022 rather than the full calendar year 2023. It is an external assessment, not an election observation mission.

### WEB-4B-02 — Electoral Plan B sought to reduce oversight capacity

**Claim.** AMLO’s electoral Plan B, approved by Congress in February 2023, would substantially reduce the INE’s budget, personnel, and office space, impairing its ability to oversee elections impartially. It followed the rejection of a broader constitutional reform that would have altered the electoral system and appointment rules.

**Why it matters.** This is the clearest 2023 evidence of a proposed institutional change that could have weakened genuine electoral contestability and future checks on executive power.

**Source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024. [BTI PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf)

**Locator.** Pages 8 and 10–13: Plan B’s budget, personnel, and office-space effects; pressure on INE, TEPJF, INAI, and other autonomous institutions.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 legislative evidence, with the broader proposal originating in 2022.

**Attribution.** Strong. Plan B was an AMLO administration initiative supported by the MORENA-led congressional coalition.

**Complications.** The government argued that reform would reduce bureaucracy and establish a more authentic democracy. The original constitutional reform failed because the coalition lacked the required supermajority.

**Cautions.** BTI is an expert comparative assessment. The described effects concern the proposed law and should be paired with the enacted text and judicial ruling.

### WEB-4B-03 — Supreme Court invalidated Plan B

**Claim.** The Supreme Court invalidated the electoral Plan B package because Congress had failed to comply with required publication and deliberation procedures. The decision restored the prior electoral rules.

**Why it matters.** The ruling demonstrates that courts remained capable of constraining the president and congressional majority, even amid public attacks on judicial independence.

**Source.** Supreme Court of Justice, communiqué on the invalidation of the electoral reform package, 2023. [SCJN](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408)

**Locator.** Communiqué: the reforms were presented and approved without being properly published before the legislative session; the prior rules therefore resumed effect.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong for the attempted reform and strong for the institutional check; the Court’s decision is not evidence of AMLO’s voluntary restraint.

**Complications.** The Court’s decision was procedural. It did not necessarily resolve every substantive concern about electoral administration.

**Cautions.** The direct SCJN page was difficult to decode during inspection; the fact is also preserved in the supplied catalogue and corroborated by BTI and contemporaneous reporting.

### WEB-4B-04 — Pressure on courts, electoral bodies, and autonomous institutions

**Claim.** AMLO repeatedly criticized the INE, TEPJF, Supreme Court, and autonomous oversight bodies. BTI reported that he undermined judicial autonomy, that autonomous institutions had been weakened or politically captured, and that new INE appointments included several commissioners with close MORENA ties.

**Why it matters.** Democratic resilience depends not only on formal institutions but on whether the executive accepts adverse decisions and protects institutional autonomy.

**Source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024. [BTI PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf)

**Locator.** Pages 10–13, especially lines concerning the Supreme Court, INAI, INE, TEPJF, commissioner appointments, and presidential attacks.

**Period/status.** Broader AMLO-period condition continuing into 2023.

**Attribution.** Strong for presidential rhetoric and executive-political pressure; moderate for institutional capture, which involved Congress, appointment processes, and autonomous bodies.

**Complications.** The Supreme Court invalidated Plan B, and the INE and TEPJF continued to administer and adjudicate elections.

**Cautions.** BTI’s language about capture and presidential intent is evaluative. Appointment-by-appointment evidence should be checked against official nomination and confirmation records.

### WEB-4B-05 — Media remained active but faced pressure and violence

**Claim.** Mexico retained formally free and pluralistic media, but journalists faced killings, attacks, self-censorship, criminal intimidation, and official discrediting. AMLO’s “mañanera” repeatedly labeled critical journalists as conservative, corrupt, or biased; the “Who’s Who of Lies” segment was criticized by the IACHR Special Rapporteur.

**Why it matters.** Independent information and safe criticism are central to political freedom. Violence by criminal groups does not eliminate federal responsibility for protection, investigation, and presidential rhetoric.

**Sources.**

- *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)
- *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung. [BTI PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf)
- *Mexico Archives*, Committee to Protect Journalists. [CPJ](https://cpj.org/americas/mexico/2023/)

**Locator.** Freedom House, media section, lines 110–117; BTI, pp. 9–10; CPJ 2023 statements concerning attacks on journalists and AMLO’s criticism of media organizations.

**Period/status.** Inherited pattern continuing during 2023.

**Attribution.** Moderate. Criminal groups caused many physical attacks; AMLO’s public rhetoric and the federal protection/investigative environment were directly attributable.

**Complications.** Independent media continued operating, and media pluralism remained substantial. The government did not impose general formal censorship.

**Cautions.** Public criticism is not itself proof of direct incitement or state violence. Attribution for individual journalist killings is often uncertain.

### WEB-4B-06 — Spyware allegations and surveillance pressure

**Claim.** In 2023, civil-society researchers and international media reported that Pegasus spyware had targeted figures investigating military abuses, including Undersecretary Alejandro Encinas. The INAI ordered SEDENA to search for and disclose contracts related to Pegasus, while AMLO denied or minimized the allegations. Freedom House also reported that Citizen Lab identified other surveillance technology contracted by government clients in Mexico.

**Why it matters.** Surveillance of journalists, activists, or officials involved in accountability work can suppress criticism and weaken independent checks.

**Source.** *Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023)

**Locator.** Lines 417–427: Encinas targeting, presidential denials, INAI order, planned congressional investigation, Citizen Lab reporting on QuaDream and other surveillance systems.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 evidence with earlier surveillance history.

**Attribution.** Strong for presidential denials and federal transparency obligations; moderate for the underlying spyware use because technical attribution relied on Citizen Lab, investigative reporting, and leaked or forensic evidence.

**Complications.** The administration denied unlawful spying and characterized military activity as intelligence. INAI and Congress provided potential oversight mechanisms.

**Cautions.** The Freedom House report is a secondary synthesis. Technical and legal conclusions should be checked against the underlying Citizen Lab, R3D, ARTICLE 19, and court records.

### WEB-4B-07 — Internet remained open but propaganda and removal-request transparency were concerns

**Claim.** Mexico did not impose nationwide internet shutdowns or broad website blocking in the 2023 coverage period, and online political mobilization remained robust. However, Freedom House reported opaque government content-removal requests, alleged use of public resources to influence online discourse, and a proposed Federal Cybersecurity Law that could expand cyber-police monitoring.

**Why it matters.** This provides important contrary evidence against a simple censorship narrative while documenting subtler forms of digital pressure and propaganda.

**Source.** *Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023)

**Locator.** Lines 31–32; 104–107; 167–168; 246; 429: no government-imposed connectivity restrictions, opaque removal requests, alleged official propaganda apparatus, and proposed cyber-surveillance powers.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 coverage, with some cited practices inherited from earlier years.

**Attribution.** Moderate-to-strong for proposed legislation and executive information policy; weaker for alleged online manipulation because the report relies on investigative and civil-society findings.

**Complications.** No nationwide shutdowns or general encryption restrictions were reported; the internet remained a significant space for dissent.

**Cautions.** The report’s coverage period and cited examples do not establish that every alleged propaganda activity was centrally directed by AMLO.

### WEB-4B-08 — Gender representation improved; Indigenous and Afro-Mexican representation remained limited

**Claim.** Mexico’s electoral rules produced substantial gender representation: after the 2021 elections, women held approximately half of the Chamber of Deputies and Senate, and women won six governorships. Indigenous and Afro-Mexican people, however, remained severely underrepresented in national political institutions.

**Why it matters.** Equal political rights require both formal access and meaningful representation of historically excluded groups.

**Source.** *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House, 2023. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)

**Locator.** Political-rights section, lines 84–86.

**Period/status.** Baseline and ruler-period evidence; the representation figures derive from the 2021 elections.

**Attribution.** Low-to-moderate personal attribution. The outcome reflects constitutional and electoral rules developed across administrations, though AMLO’s government operated within them.

**Complications.** Gender parity was a significant democratic inclusion success. Underrepresentation was not necessarily caused by AMLO.

**Cautions.** The source does not provide a full 2023 group-access analysis or distinguish federal from state-level barriers.

### WEB-4B-09 — Military expansion and criminal influence weakened democratic accountability

**Claim.** Organized crime constrained elected authority in some regions, while the federal government expanded military responsibilities into public security, infrastructure, and economic administration. Freedom House and BTI identified risks to civilian accountability and democratic control.

**Why it matters.** Political freedom can be undermined without formal dictatorship when elected officials, journalists, and local institutions face criminal coercion or when security institutions become politically and economically entrenched.

**Sources.**

- *Mexico: Freedom in the World 2023 Country Report*, Freedom House. [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-world/2023)
- *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*, Bertelsmann Stiftung. [BTI PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf)

**Locator.** Freedom House lines 77–80 and 91–103; BTI pp. 8–13.

**Period/status.** Ongoing AMLO-period condition, partly inherited.

**Attribution.** Strong for military expansion; moderate for criminal influence, which was not solely produced by federal policy.

**Complications.** The military was used to address genuine security and administrative-capacity problems, and courts retained some power to constrain its role.

**Cautions.** The claim concerns institutional risk, not proof that Mexico had already become military rule or authoritarian government.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **4B.1 — Genuine electoral choice:** Mixed. Competitive elections and opposition viability remained, but Plan B threatened electoral-administration capacity before judicial invalidation.
- **4B.10 — Lasting democratic resilience:** Mixed-to-adverse. Courts and elections remained resilient, but pressure on oversight institutions, media, and autonomous bodies created longer-term risks.
- **4B.2 — No entrenchment of power:** Adverse evidence exists. Plan B and attacks on electoral institutions could have advantaged the governing coalition; no evidence shows AMLO changed term limits or directly prevented succession.
- **4B.3 — Opposition and civic freedom:** Mixed-to-adverse. Opposition parties and civil society operated, but journalists, protesters, activists, and critics faced public stigmatization, violence, or surveillance risks.
- **4B.4 — Independent checks on power:** Mixed. The Supreme Court invalidated Plan B, but the executive criticized courts and sought to weaken INE, TEPJF, INAI, and other autonomous bodies.
- **4B.5 — Politically neutral institutions:** Adverse and unresolved. BTI reported politically connected appointments and weakened autonomous institutions; appointment-level verification remains incomplete.
- **4B.6 — Independent information and media:** Mixed-to-adverse. Formal censorship was limited and media remained pluralistic, but violence, official attacks, propaganda allegations, advertising dependence, and surveillance concerns impaired independence.
- **4B.7 — Equal political rights:** Mixed. Gender representation was a major positive outcome; Indigenous and Afro-Mexican representation remained inadequate, and political violence affected local candidates and groups unevenly.
- **4B.8 — Term limits and transfer:** Supported for the target year. AMLO remained constitutionally barred from reelection and did not alter the presidential term. The actual 2024 transfer was outside the requested period.
- **4B.9 — Digital freedom and surveillance:** Mixed-to-adverse. No general shutdowns or website blocking were reported, but spyware allegations, opaque content-removal requests, propaganda claims, and proposed cyber-monitoring powers raised significant concerns.

## 4. Information environment

Information was relatively abundant but politically polarized. Formal laws, court records, electoral assessments, and digital-rights reports were available. The main limitations were:

- government and opposition sources framed Plan B and institutional reform in sharply conflicting terms;
- official presidential rhetoric was abundant but self-serving;
- some electoral and appointment records required direct legal-document inspection;
- spyware allegations relied on technical investigations and leaked or forensic evidence;
- threats to journalists and criminal control created self-censorship, especially outside major cities;
- Freedom House and BTI often assessed the broader 2022–2023 period rather than calendar year 2023 alone.

The strongest evidence of institutional restraint is the Supreme Court’s invalidation of Plan B and the continued existence of competitive elections. The strongest evidence of democratic erosion is the combination of proposed electoral weakening, attacks on oversight institutions, presidential pressure on journalists and courts, military expansion, and surveillance controversies.

- Amnesty document: fetch error.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

At least 15 candidates were inspected or attempted. Eight yielded accepted evidence; several others supplied corroboration or were blocked.

Remaining research gaps:

1. Exact enacted text and implementation consequences of Plan B.
2. Full Supreme Court reasoning and whether any substantive constitutional issues were decided.
3. Appointment-by-appointment evidence for INE, INAI, judiciary, and other autonomous bodies.
4. Actual 2023 funding and staffing changes affecting INE, INAI, TEPJF, and civil-society oversight.
5. Evidence of AMLO’s influence over MORENA candidate selection and the 2024 succession process.
6. Case-level evidence connecting presidential rhetoric to threats or violence against journalists and protesters.
7. Technical and legal findings on Pegasus and QuaDream use during 2023.
8. Whether criminal control of local politics materially altered voter choice in specific states or municipalities.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# Economic Well-Being and Prosperity — Andrés Manuel López Obrador, Mexico, 2023

## 1. Orientation

AMLO controlled federal fiscal policy, public investment, welfare budgets, labor policy, Pemex support, and the main development agenda. Monetary policy remained formally independent under Banco de México. Congress retained budgetary authority, while courts, the ASF, CONEVAL, market conditions, the USMCA framework, global interest rates, oil prices, and state governments constrained implementation.

Mexico’s 2023 economy grew strongly by recent standards: Banco de México reported real GDP growth of 3.2%. Employment and real wages improved, and official multidimensional poverty had fallen between 2018 and 2022. However, the period also featured high inflation earlier in the adjustment cycle, large informal employment, persistent regional inequality, health-access losses, weak Pemex finances, rising debt-service costs, and concerns about the quality and transparency of public investment.

The central economic tension was between AMLO’s redistributive and state-led model and its implementation weaknesses. Welfare transfers and minimum-wage increases produced measurable distributional gains, while fiscal decisions, Pemex support, infrastructure cost escalation, and reductions in some social-service budgets raised questions about long-term sustainability and opportunity cost.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-5B-01 | Mexico grew 3.2% in 2023 amid recovery and external constraints | 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.9, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-02 | Poverty fell between 2018 and 2022, but health access and deprivation problems remained | 5B.1, 5B.8, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-03 | Labor poverty improved and minimum-wage policy raised labor incomes | 5B.1, 5B.4, 5B.8, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-04 | Welfare budgets expanded substantially in 2023 | 5B.1, 5B.6, 5B.8 |
| WEB-5B-05 | Health and some social-program budgets were reduced or unevenly funded | 5B.6, 5B.8, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-06 | Fiscal revenues rose but missed targets; debt service and Pemex liabilities increased | 5B.3, 5B.5, 5B.7, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-07 | Flagship infrastructure advanced but faced major delays and cost escalation | 5B.1, 5B.5, 5B.6, 5B.7 |
| WEB-5B-08 | Economic leadership preserved monetary credibility but relied on a politically directed investment model | 5B.2, 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.10 |

### WEB-5B-01 — 2023 growth and macroeconomic recovery

**Claim.** Mexico’s real GDP increased approximately 3.2% in 2023. Banco de México attributed the macroeconomic picture to continued expansion, while the World Bank and official sources identified recovery from the pandemic, domestic demand, construction, and external conditions as relevant factors.

**Why it matters.** This is the principal aggregate prosperity outcome, but it does not by itself establish improvements in household welfare or the quality of the growth path.

**Sources.**

- *Executive Summary: Quarterly Report October–December 2023*, Banco de México, 2024. [Banco de México PDF](https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/quarterly-reports/%7BC3A8F458-B597-DD1E-51A3-22C6D18BBEFB%7D.pdf)
- World Bank, *GDP growth (annual %) — Mexico*. [World Bank data](https://data.worldbank.org/indicator/NY.GDP.MKTP.KD.ZG?locations=MX&start=2023)

**Locator.** Banco de México search result: “Mexico’s GDP increased 3.2% in 2023”; World Bank WDI annual growth series.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year outcome.

**Attribution.** Moderate. Federal policy influenced demand, transfers, construction, and confidence, but growth also reflected post-pandemic normalization, US demand, remittances, and global conditions.

**Complications.** Growth was positive and broad enough to support employment gains, but per-capita and distributional outcomes require separate evidence.

**Cautions.** GDP growth is not a measure of median living standards, inequality, environmental cost, or fiscal sustainability.

### WEB-5B-02 — Poverty reduction with continuing health-access and deprivation problems

**Claim.** Mexico’s official multidimensional poverty rate declined from 41.9% in 2018 to 36.3% in 2022, according to CONEVAL reporting summarized in the 2023 evidence catalogue. The reduction coincided with improved labor income and transfers, but health-service deprivation increased substantially, and regional poverty remained concentrated in southern and rural areas.

**Why it matters.** This is stronger evidence of household welfare than GDP alone, while the health-access deterioration complicates a simple claim of broad-based prosperity.

**Sources.**

- World Bank, *Poverty & Equity Brief: Mexico*, April 2023. [World Bank PDF](https://databankfiles.worldbank.org/public/ddpext_download/poverty/987B9C90-CB9F-4D93-AE8C-750588BF00QA/current/Global_POVEQ_MEX.pdf)
- CONEVAL evaluation and poverty materials. [CONEVAL evaluation portal](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Evaluacion/IPE/Paginas/Busqueda.aspx?pEstId=15)

**Locator.** World Bank brief, pp. 1–2: 2020 poverty, inequality, regional disparities, employment, and health/education deprivations. The 2018–2022 official poverty comparison requires the corresponding CONEVAL release.

**Period/status.** Outcome through 2022, relevant baseline for 2023; 2023 full multidimensional poverty data were not yet available in the inspected source.

**Attribution.** Moderate. Transfers, labor policy, and post-pandemic recovery were partly attributable to AMLO; inflation, labor-market normalization, and health-system disruption also mattered.

**Complications.** Poverty reduction was substantial and not merely claimed by the government; the World Bank independently documented improving labor poverty and employment recovery.

**Cautions.** The World Bank brief uses 2020 household data and warns about survey comparability. Health-access changes require direct CONEVAL 2022 tables.

### WEB-5B-03 — Minimum-wage increases and labor-income improvement

**Claim.** Minimum-wage increases during the AMLO period contributed to higher labor incomes, and labor poverty had returned to approximately its pre-pandemic level by late 2022. However, informal employment remained around 56%, and the share of jobs paying one minimum wage or less was higher than before the pandemic.

**Why it matters.** This distinguishes real distributional gains from the quality and security of employment.

**Sources.**

- *Efecto del aumento del salario mínimo sobre el ingreso laboral en México, 2018–2023*, UNAM, *Investigación Económica*. [UNAM](https://revistas.unam.mx/index.php/rie/article/view/89883)
- World Bank, *Poverty & Equity Brief: Mexico*, April 2023. [World Bank PDF](https://databankfiles.worldbank.org/public/ddpext_download/poverty/987B9C90-CB9F-4D93-AE8C-750588BF00QA/current/Global_POVEQ_MEX.pdf)
- CONEVAL, *Pobreza laboral al cuarto trimestre de 2023*. [CONEVAL](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/Paginas/Pobreza_laboral_4toTrim2023.aspx)

**Locator.** World Bank brief, p. 1: labor poverty, informality, and low-wage employment; UNAM article title and methodology concern the 2018–2023 minimum-wage period; CONEVAL fourth-quarter 2023 publication provides the target-year labor-poverty series.

**Period/status.** Direct ruler-period and target-year labor evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong for the minimum-wage policy, which was implemented through federal wage-setting institutions; moderate for total labor-income outcomes because growth, remittances, and labor-market recovery also mattered.

**Complications.** Wage increases did not produce the feared large employment collapse in the cited literature, and labor poverty improved.

**Cautions.** The UNAM article’s accessible page did not expose the full abstract or all estimates. Informality and job quality remain major limitations.

### WEB-5B-04 — Welfare-budget expansion

**Claim.** CONEVAL’s analysis of the 2023 federal budget found that programs in the Welfare ministry increased 39.5% relative to 2022, while the main old-age pension increased 42.6%, from approximately 238.0 billion to 339.3 billion pesos.

**Why it matters.** This documents a major resource choice at the center of AMLO’s redistribution strategy.

**Source.** *Análisis del PEF 2022–2023*, CONEVAL. [CONEVAL PDF](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/MejorasUso/IPP/Documents/ANALISIS_PEF_2022-2023_IE.pdf)

**Locator.** pp. 22–23, lines 714–746: Welfare-sector increase and old-age pension budget.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 budget evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong. The federal executive proposed the budget and AMLO’s governing coalition approved it.

**Complications.** Direct transfers may reduce administrative intermediaries and provide income support; the budget increase does not establish targeting quality, long-term adequacy, or causal poverty reduction by itself.

**Cautions.** This is an analysis of approved budgets, not audited execution or outcome evaluation. It does not measure leakage, take-up, or distribution across households.

### WEB-5B-05 — Uneven social-program and health funding

**Claim.** CONEVAL reported that the 2023 budget for IMSS-BIENESTAR fell 12.9% from 2022, while some health programs and disability-related programs also faced reductions or uneven changes. This contrasted with large increases in pensions and some other welfare programs.

**Why it matters.** It shows that redistribution involved prioritization and trade-offs, not simply generalized social investment.

**Source.** *Análisis del PEF 2022–2023*, CONEVAL. [CONEVAL PDF](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/MejorasUso/IPP/Documents/ANALISIS_PEF_2022-2023_IE.pdf)

**Locator.** p. 21, lines 693–712: IMSS-BIENESTAR budget reduction; pp. 27–28, lines 943–996: IMSS and ISSSTE program changes.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 budget evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong for budget priorities; weaker for actual service outcomes because execution and local implementation varied.

**Complications.** Other health and social programs received increases, and budget classification changes may complicate direct comparisons.

**Cautions.** Approved-budget change is not the same as real spending or service availability. The source does not establish that AMLO intended to reduce health access.

### WEB-5B-06 — Fiscal performance, debt service, and Pemex exposure

**Claim.** IMCO and Mexico Evalúa reported that public-sector revenues rose 1% in real terms in 2023 but fell 1.2% below target; oil revenues fell 31% from 2022; debt service exceeded one trillion pesos and represented 3.3% of GDP; and only 65 cents of each peso of new financing went to physical investment.

**Why it matters.** These figures raise questions about macroeconomic sustainability, fiscal transparency, the opportunity cost of debt, and the administration’s support for Pemex.

**Source.** *Comunicado: resultados de finanzas públicas 2023*, IMCO and Mexico Evalúa, 21 February 2024. [IMCO PDF](https://imco.org.mx/wp-content/uploads/2024/02/Finanzas-publicas-2023__Comunicado_20240221.pdf)

**Locator.** pp. 1–3, lines 7–18, 24–33, 52–64, and 136–145.

**Period/status.** Direct 2023 fiscal outcome.

**Attribution.** Strong for federal fiscal choices, Pemex support, and debt authorization; moderate for oil-revenue decline because international prices and exchange rates were external factors.

**Complications.** Tax revenue reached a historic high and public debt remained manageable by some international comparisons. Some borrowing financed infrastructure rather than purely current expenditure.

**Cautions.** IMCO and Mexico Evalúa are independent policy organizations with a critical fiscal perspective. Their conclusions should be paired with SHCP and Auditoría Superior records.

### WEB-5B-07 — Flagship infrastructure advanced amid cost escalation

**Claim.** The Maya Train opened partially in December 2023 after delays and incomplete infrastructure. AP reported that the project’s original projected cost was approximately $8.6 billion, while $22.7 billion had been assigned by the opening and the Treasury secretary estimated a final cost near $28 billion.

**Why it matters.** The Maya Train was a central AMLO development project and a test of productive public investment, procurement, implementation, and fiscal discipline.

**Source.** *Mexico’s Maya tourist train opens for partial service amid delays and cost overruns*, Associated Press, 16 December 2023. [AP](https://apnews.com/article/7b0db1b6b262acde6721debceaa5af7e)

**Locator.** AP report: partial opening, hours-long delays, incomplete double track, original and revised cost figures, and AMLO’s acknowledgment that the project was not complete.

**Period/status.** Direct target-year evidence.

**Attribution.** Strong. The project was an AMLO flagship, publicly defended by him, and administered partly by the army.

**Complications.** The train did begin service and could yield future tourism, regional-development, and transport benefits. Cost figures were not yet final.

**Cautions.** AP relied partly on government cost statements. Final project cost, operating subsidies, and distributional effects require ASF and financial records.

### WEB-5B-08 — Qualified economic leadership and macroeconomic independence

**Claim.** Banco de México continued to publish independent monetary and economic analysis, and Mexico’s 2023 growth and inflation data were measured through the central-bank and INEGI statistical system. At the same time, independent assessments described AMLO’s investment decisions as politically guided and his government as more interventionist in energy and infrastructure than in macroeconomic management.

**Why it matters.** Economic leadership should distinguish professional monetary administration from presidential control over fiscal and investment policy.

**Sources.**

- Banco de México, *Informe trimestral octubre–diciembre 2023*. [Banco de México](https://www.banxico.org.mx/publicaciones-y-prensa/resumenes-visuales/informe-trimestral-html/informe-trimestral-oct-dic-00002.html)
- Banco de México, inflation series. [SIE](https://www.banxico.org.mx/SieInternet/consultarDirectorioInternetAction.do?accion=consultarCuadro&idCuadro=CP151&sector=8)
- *BTI 2024 Country Report — Mexico*. [BTI PDF](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf)

**Locator.** Banco de México report and inflation-series pages; BTI pp. 2–4 on politically guided investment, macroeconomic continuity, and energy policy.

**Period/status.** Target-year macroeconomic administration with broader ruler-period assessment.

**Attribution.** Strong for preservation of central-bank operational processes; strong for presidential direction of infrastructure and energy choices; lower for individual ministerial competence without appointment records.

**Complications.** Mexico retained credible monetary institutions and did not experience a currency or debt crisis in 2023.

**Cautions.** BTI’s characterization of political motives is evaluative. Banco de México’s independence limits direct presidential attribution for inflation and interest-rate outcomes.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **5B.1 — Broad and sustainable prosperity:** Mixed. Growth, wage gains, poverty reduction, and expanded transfers support a broad-benefit account; Pemex exposure, weak investment quality, informality, and regional inequality complicate it.
- **5B.10 — Lasting economic trajectory:** Mixed and unresolved. The economy grew and household indicators improved, but debt service, health-access problems, Pemex liabilities, and infrastructure cost escalation create sustainability concerns.
- **5B.2 — Qualified economic leadership:** Partly supported. Banco de México retained professional independence; stronger evidence is needed on AMLO’s cabinet appointments, Treasury delegation, and replacement decisions.
- **5B.3 — Macroeconomic stability:** Generally supported for 2023, with significant fiscal concerns. There was no macroeconomic crisis, but debt-service costs and financing composition deteriorated.
- **5B.4 — Fair economic rules:** Mixed. Minimum-wage and labor reforms improved worker bargaining power, while energy policy, uncertainty, and politically directed investment raised predictability concerns.
- **5B.5 — Resistance to economic capture:** Adverse evidence is concentrated in Pemex support, direct contracting, military-run projects, and weak procurement transparency. Final ASF findings remain necessary.
- **5B.6 — Productive public investment:** Mixed-to-adverse. Large infrastructure projects advanced, but the Maya Train’s delays and cost escalation raise implementation and value-for-money concerns.
- **5B.7 — Evidence and correction:** Mixed. CONEVAL, Banco de México, ASF, IMCO, and Mexico Evalúa continued producing evaluations and criticism; the administration’s response to adverse findings and revisions requires deeper inspection.
- **5B.8 — Fair distribution:** Mixed-to-positive. Minimum wages and pensions improved income distribution for many beneficiaries, but informality, regional poverty, health-access losses, and uneven program incidence remained.
- **5B.9 — Managing economic shocks:** Mixed. Inflation was managed without macroeconomic breakdown, and employment recovered, but earlier food-price pressures and uneven public-service funding affected poorer households disproportionately.

## 4. Information environment

Economic information was comparatively strong: Banco de México, INEGI, CONEVAL, SHCP, World Bank, and the ASF provided substantial statistical and fiscal material. Limitations remained:

- full 2023 poverty data were released with lags;
- approved budgets did not always equal executed spending;
- GDP did not reveal household distribution or job quality;
- official project cost figures changed over time;
- government claims about welfare and infrastructure outcomes were promotional;
- independent fiscal organizations emphasized risks and may differ in methodology;
- state-level implementation and regional incidence were difficult to isolate.

The safest interpretation is to separate four layers: policy announcement, approved resources, actual execution, and measured household or macroeconomic outcome.

## 6. Source counts and remaining gaps

At least 15 candidates were inspected or attempted. Ten yielded accepted evidence; several others supplied context or were blocked.

Remaining gaps:

1. Executed rather than approved 2023 spending.
2. Final poverty and labor-poverty distribution by region, gender, and household type.
3. Direct causal estimates for transfers and minimum-wage increases.
4. Full 2023 inflation burden by consumption basket.
5. Final Maya Train and Pemex audit findings.
6. Appointment and independence records for Treasury, central-bank, competition, and regulatory officials.
7. Whether infrastructure spending produced measurable productivity or regional-income gains.
8. Whether fiscal borrowing displaced social, health, or education investment.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# 6B — Social Well-Being and Human Development

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador was Mexico’s constitutional president in 2023 and exercised strong control over federal social policy, budgets, and public messaging. His government prioritized cash transfers, pensions, and state-led social programs, while Congress, courts, autonomous evaluators, state governments, administrative capacity, and fiscal limits constrained implementation.

The evidence shows a mixed record: poverty and child poverty fell by 2022 after the pandemic shock, and welfare budgets expanded substantially in 2023. However, health-service deprivation rose sharply, IMSS-BIENESTAR funding fell in the 2023 budget proposal, children remained poorer than the general population, and service quality and institutional capacity were uneven. Most outcome data available in 2023 describe 2022, so attribution to policy choices must be cautious.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-6B-01 — Poverty declined, but social deprivation remained extensive

CONEVAL reported that poverty fell from 43.9% in 2020 to 36.3% in 2022, or 46.8 million people. Yet 65.7% of the population still experienced at least one social deprivation, including 39.1% lacking access to health services and 50.2% lacking social security.

Source: [Medición multidimensional de la pobreza en México 2022](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf), CONEVAL, August 2023, pp. 9–14 and 21–24.

Status: 2022 outcome published during the target year. Attribution to AMLO: moderate; transfers and labor-market recovery may have contributed, but the result also reflects post-pandemic recovery and other factors.

### WEB-6B-02 — Welfare and old-age pension budgets expanded

CONEVAL’s analysis of the 2023 federal budget found that the Welfare Ministry’s social-program budget increased 39.5%, while the old-age pension budget rose 42.6%.

Source: [Análisis del PEF 2022-2023](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/MejorasUso/IPP/Documents/ANALISIS_PEF_2022-2023_IE.pdf), CONEVAL, 2023, pp. 21–22.

Status: 2023 authorized-budget evidence. Attribution: strong for federal policy priority, weaker for actual delivery and welfare outcomes.

### WEB-6B-03 — Health financing and access were uneven

The same CONEVAL budget analysis recorded a 12.9% reduction in the IMSS-BIENESTAR program budget between 2022 and 2023, despite the administration’s effort to centralize and expand public health provision. CONEVAL’s poverty measurement simultaneously recorded health-service deprivation rising to 39.1% in 2022, from 28.2% in 2020.

Status: 2023 budget plus 2022 outcome. Attribution: strong for federal budget choices and institutional redesign; causal attribution for the access decline remains contested.

### WEB-6B-04 — Mexico retained an independent measurement and evaluation institution

CONEVAL described its statutory mandate under the General Social Development Law to measure poverty, evaluate social policy, publish disaggregated results, and make methods publicly replicable. Its 2022 poverty report used ENIGH data, internal validation, external comparison, and public algorithms.

Status: inherited and continuing institution during 2023. Attribution: mixed. AMLO’s government operated within this framework, but did not create CONEVAL; the institution provided an important independent check on official social-policy claims.

### WEB-6B-05 — Children remained substantially poorer than the general population

UNICEF reported that child and adolescent poverty fell from 52.6% in 2020 to 45.8% in 2022, but remained above the national rate. Approximately 17 million children and adolescents were still living in poverty with multiple social deprivations.

Source: [Informe Anual 2023 y Un buen sexenio para la niñez](https://www.unicef.org/mexico/informes/informeanual2023), UNICEF Mexico, August 2024, sections “Informe Anual 2023” and “Un Buen Sexenio para la Niñez.”

Status: later retrospective using 2022 outcomes. Attribution: moderate; it assesses the period but does not isolate federal policy effects.

### WEB-6B-06 — UNICEF supported the government after Hurricane Otis

UNICEF reported that after Hurricane Otis it supported federal, state, and municipal authorities and that 32,727 people benefited from its interventions. The response covered child health, education, protection, and social services.

Status: 2023 crisis response. Attribution to AMLO: limited to federal coordination and the national emergency framework; UNICEF was an independent international partner and a major implementing actor.

### WEB-6B-07 — Health and human-development data remained temporally incomplete

WHO’s country profile shows that several relevant Mexico indicators available in 2023 or later were based on older observations: health expenditure data from 2021, life expectancy and causes of death from 2021, and water, sanitation, staffing, and nutrition indicators mainly from 2022. The profile also notes that some indicators are modeled estimates.

Status: evidence-environment/context record. It limits conclusions about 2023 outcomes.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **6B.1 Welfare as a governing purpose — Evidence supports a genuine priority, with patronage concerns unresolved.** WEB-6B-02 and WEB-6B-03 show large welfare and pension commitments, but budget expansion alone does not establish effectiveness or political neutrality.
- **6B.2 Access to essential services — Mixed and adverse evidence.** WEB-6B-01 and WEB-6B-03 show substantial health-access deterioration despite broader social spending.
- **6B.3 Priority for vulnerable groups — Partial support, significant remaining disadvantage.** WEB-6B-05 shows child poverty declined but remained much higher than the national rate.
- **6B.4 Professional service delivery — Inconclusive/mixed.** Budget restructuring and health-system centralization are documented, but reliable 2023 staffing, procurement, and service-quality evidence was not fully accessible.
- **6B.5 Measurement and correction — Relatively strong institutional evidence.** WEB-6B-04 documents CONEVAL’s independent statutory measurement and evaluation role; evidence of government correction in response to findings is limited.
- **6B.6 Protection during crises — Partial support.** WEB-6B-06 documents coordinated post-Otis assistance, but a full assessment requires emergency preparedness, reconstruction, and distribution data.
- **6B.7 No political allocation of welfare — Unresolved.** The sources reviewed establish programs and budgets but do not adequately test partisan targeting, conditionality, or appeals.
- **6B.8 Dignity and equal opportunity — Mixed.** Persistent child poverty and health-access inequality indicate incomplete equal opportunity; disability, Indigenous, gender, and regional incidence require deeper inspection.
- **6B.9 Durable social institutions — Mixed.** CONEVAL provides durable evaluation infrastructure, but health-system restructuring and reliance on executive programs raise questions about institutional durability.
- **6B.10 Lasting life chances — Mixed, no definitive conclusion.** Poverty improved by 2022, but health access worsened and major subgroup disparities persisted.

## 4. Sources fully extracted and retained

- [CONEVAL, Medición multidimensional de la pobreza en México 2022](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf) — accepted evidence.
- [CONEVAL, Análisis del PEF 2022-2023](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/MejorasUso/IPP/Documents/ANALISIS_PEF_2022-2023_IE.pdf) — accepted evidence.
- [UNICEF Mexico, Informe Anual 2023 y Un buen sexenio para la niñez](https://www.unicef.org/mexico/informes/informeanual2023) — accepted evidence and later retrospective.
- [WHO, Mexico country data](https://data.who.int/countries/484) — accepted context evidence.
- [CONEVAL, program evaluation materials](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/PP/Programas/Integral_Desempeno/Paginas/Resumen_Desempeno_2022.aspx) — corroboration/context.

## 5. Sources opened mainly for corroboration

- [World Bank Mexico data](https://data.worldbank.org/country/mexico).
- [CONEVAL evaluation and monitoring publications](https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/InformesPublicaciones/Paginas/Publicaciones-sobre-Evaluacion-y-monitoreo.aspx).
- [Auditoría Superior de la Federación, 2023 audit repository](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/index.html).
- [CONEVAL integrated program-performance summary](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/PP/Programas/Integral_Desempeno/Paginas/Resumen_Desempeno_2022.aspx).

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- ENSANUT Continua 2023: [INSP report portal](https://ensanut.insp.mx/encuestas/ensanutcontinua2023/informes.php).
- IMSS-BIENESTAR audit: [ASF audit 2023_0409_a](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0409_a.pdf).
- [CEDAW Mexico materials](https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4073547/files/CEDAW_C_MEX_10-EN.pdf).
- [UN Committee on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities observations](https://uhri.ohchr.org/Document/File/8a7e638f-259f-404b-b1f7-4851be144c86/F8AB3743-9071-49E7-837C-6C2F5018FCD2).
- [CONEVAL 2023 evaluation results](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/PP/DOF/Paginas/2023.aspx).
- Indigenous, disability, education, and program-operation records listed in the supplied catalogue.

Source count: 5 accepted evidence sources, 4 corroborating/context sources, 6 promising leads, and 10+ additional candidates opened or attempted. Main gaps concern 2023 service delivery, regional and subgroup incidence, procurement, staffing, political conditionality, and independent evaluation of health-system reform.
