--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# 7B — Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador held the presidency and exercised unusually strong personal control over the federal political agenda, party messaging, and daily public communication. His central ethical claim was that “republican austerity” and personal honesty would replace corruption and elite privilege.

The 2023 record is mixed. There is no established judicial finding that AMLO personally enriched himself or accepted bribes. However, credible evidence documents major corruption within agencies under his administration, unresolved conflicts involving close associates and family members, pressure on transparency institutions, and repeated confrontational responses to journalists and investigators. The strongest evidence supports institutional and accountability concerns more clearly than personal criminal culpability.

## 2. Evidence index

### WEB-7B-01 — Transparency complaints increased and INAI was weakened

In April 2023, *El País* reported that complaints against government responses to information requests had risen 19% compared with the comparable period of the previous administration. It reported that many authorities claimed records did not exist or classified them as national security information. INAI also lacked a quorum after the Senate failed to complete appointments; AMLO had vetoed two approved nominees.

This matters because independent access to records is essential for detecting conflicts, procurement abuse, and concealed decisions.

Source: [“Las quejas por falta de transparencia se disparan durante el Gobierno de López Obrador”](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-04-24/los-quejas-por-falta-de-transparencia-se-disparan-durante-el-gobierno-de-lopez-obrador.html), *El País México*, 24 April 2023, paragraphs 1–9.

Status: target-period. Attribution: strong for the president’s public hostility toward INAI and direct vetoes; broader transparency failures involve multiple agencies and the Senate.

### WEB-7B-02 — Segalmex corruption became a major unresolved integrity failure

Audits and legislative records concerning Seguridad Alimentaria Mexicana (Segalmex) documented suspected irregularities involving billions of pesos. The scandal concerned procurement, contracts, and alleged diversion or misuse of public funds under an agency created by the AMLO administration. AMLO publicly defended his anti-corruption record while the case remained under investigation.

This is strong evidence of corruption and accountability failure within the ruler’s administration, but not proof that AMLO personally profited.

Sources: [ASF audit results](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Section/398_Resultados_Seccion), [Auditoría 273: adquisiciones de Segalmex](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0273_a.pdf), [Auditoría 400: contratos y servicios de Segalmex](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0400_a.pdf), and [Senate legislative document](https://sil.gobernacion.gob.mx/Archivos/Documentos/2023/03/asun_4506390_20230308_1676394564.pdf).

Status: administration-period finding, with investigations continuing in 2023. Attribution: strong for agency oversight and political responsibility; weak for personal enrichment.

### WEB-7B-03 — Family and close-associate conflicts were documented but not finally adjudicated

Public allegations and legislative scrutiny concerned José Ramón López Beltrán’s relationship with a private contractor connected to a residence in Houston and possible conflicts involving close associates of the president’s family. The records establish a serious conflict-of-interest allegation and public accountability issue, but do not by themselves establish illegal benefit, bribery, or AMLO’s personal intervention.

Source: [Senate document concerning possible conflicts involving José Ramón López Beltrán](https://sil.gobernacion.gob.mx/Archivos/Documentos/2022/02/asun_4309028_20220217_1644953368.pdf), Senate of Mexico, 17 February 2022; relevant as an inherited and continuing issue during 2023.

Status: inherited controversy continuing into the target period. Attribution: indirect; the issue concerns a close family member, not an established act by AMLO.

Complication: no final judicial finding establishing that AMLO personally benefited.

### WEB-7B-04 — Investigative journalism and military accountability faced pressure

Investigations into military abuses and surveillance encountered institutional resistance. Associated Press reported that a Mexican official said the military was obstructing investigations into human-rights abuses. Civil-society and press-freedom organizations also documented presidential attacks on journalists and critics investigating military surveillance and corruption.

This matters because independent investigation is a core test of personal integrity: a ruler may not be personally implicated in every abuse, but obstruction or retaliation can protect associates and weaken accountability.

Sources: [“Mexican official says military obstructs probe into human rights abuses”](https://apnews.com/article/853cbdce6641360c7b18b0a7e4282497), Associated Press, 2023; [ARTICLE 19 Mexico resources](https://www.article19.org/resources/mexico); [CPJ Mexico documentation](https://cpj.org/americas/mexico).

Status: target-period and broader administration-period evidence. Attribution: moderate to strong for presidential rhetoric and civil-military policy; specific obstruction requires institution-level attribution.

### WEB-7B-05 — AMLO’s anti-corruption rhetoric was not consistently matched by accountability practice

AMLO repeatedly presented his administration as morally distinct from previous governments and emphasized personal honesty, austerity, and the “end of corruption.” At the same time, his administration expanded military and state-enterprise control over major projects, defended secrecy in security-related matters, and faced serious unresolved corruption scandals.

The evidence does not establish that every anti-corruption claim was false. It does establish a recurring gap between ethical rhetoric and institutional transparency.

Sources: [Quinto Informe de Gobierno](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/5-informe-de-gobierno), Presidency of Mexico, 1 September 2023; [BTI 2024 Country Report: Mexico](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf), Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, sections on corruption, transparency, and governance.

Status: target-period and later retrospective. Attribution: direct for rhetoric and federal policy choices. Caution: government reports are self-presentations and require independent corroboration.

### WEB-7B-06 — The administration accepted some formal checks but contested institutions that constrained it

The Supreme Court invalidated AMLO’s electoral “Plan B” reforms on procedural and constitutional grounds. Although electoral integrity belongs primarily to Chapter 4B, the decision is relevant here because it demonstrates that formal judicial checks operated and that the executive did not possess unlimited power.

At the same time, AMLO publicly criticized the judiciary and autonomous bodies, creating a conflict between formal compliance with some rulings and broader pressure on accountability institutions.

Source: [SCJN communiqué on invalidation of the electoral reform](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408), Supreme Court of Mexico, 2023.

Status: target-period. Attribution: direct for the executive-sponsored reform and presidential response; the court’s ruling is independent evidence.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **7B.1 Truthfulness — Mixed and disputed.** Official claims of personal honesty and anti-corruption were extensive, but independent reporting identified transparency failures and unresolved corruption scandals. No general finding that AMLO knowingly lied in every case is justified.
- **7B.2 Correcting falsehoods and errors — Adverse evidence.** The administration frequently contested investigative reporting and institutional criticism rather than acknowledging failures; the extent of formal correction and remedy varied by case.
- **7B.3 Conflicts of interest — Unresolved but serious.** Family and close-associate controversies require separation between allegation, appearance of conflict, and proven illegality.
- **7B.4 Personal profit from office — No established finding in the inspected record.** Segalmex and family controversies show exposure and accountability risks, not proof that AMLO personally profited.
- **7B.5 Nepotism and loyalist appointments — Mixed/inconclusive.** The record shows strong personal and party loyalty networks, but the inspected sources do not provide a sufficiently complete appointment-by-appointment test.
- **7B.6 Independent investigation — Adverse evidence.** INAI’s weakened capacity, military resistance to investigations, and pressure on journalists indicate reduced investigative independence.
- **7B.7 Obstruction and concealment — Substantial concern.** Increased secrecy classifications, weakened transparency institutions, and military noncooperation allegations are materially adverse; judicial and audit processes nevertheless continued.
- **7B.8 Promises and good faith — Mixed.** Anti-corruption promises were central and some formal oversight survived, but institutional practice often conflicted with the promise of maximum transparency.
- **7B.9 Favoritism and clientelism — Serious unresolved concern.** Segalmex procurement and family-associate allegations warrant deeper examination; personal direction or benefit by AMLO has not been established.
- **7B.10 Ethical example and public trust — Mixed to adverse.** AMLO’s personal austerity message was politically powerful, but attacks on critics and weakening of oversight risked normalizing low accountability.

## 4. Sources fully extracted and retained

- [El País transparency investigation](https://elpais.com/mexico/2023-04-24/los-quejas-por-falta-de-transparencia-se-disparan-durante-el-gobierno-de-lopez-obrador.html).
- [ASF Segalmex audit repository](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Section/398_Resultados_Seccion).

- [Senate Segalmex document](https://sil.gobernacion.gob.mx/Archivos/Documentos/2023/03/asun_4506390_20230308_1676394564.pdf).

- [BTI 2024 Country Report: Mexico](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf).
- [SCJN electoral-reform communiqué](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408).

## 5. Sources opened mainly for corroboration

- [U.S. Department of State Mexico human-rights report](https://2021-2025.state.gov/report/custom/5ffdf5c0e2).
- [INAI institutional portal](https://home.inai.org.mx/).
- [ARTICLE 19 Mexico resources](https://www.article19.org/resources/mexico).
- [CPJ Mexico documentation](https://cpj.org/americas/mexico).
- [Oxford Research Encyclopedia, “López Obrador in Democratic Mexico”](https://academic.oup.com/edited-volume/61800/chapter/546375326?searchresult=1).
- [UN State of Truth report](https://digitallibrary.un.org/nanna/record/4025047/files/1386775EN.pdf?registerDownload=1&version=1&withMetadata=0).

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Complete 2023 asset declarations for AMLO, cabinet members, and close associates.
- Prosecutorial case files and final judgments in the Segalmex investigations.
- Procurement databases linking Segalmex, military enterprises, and politically connected contractors.
- INAI resolutions and Senate appointment records concerning the 2023 quorum crisis.
- Detailed records on Pegasus surveillance and the president’s responses.
- Appointment records testing competence, kinship, and loyalty patterns across the cabinet and major agencies.

Source count: 10 accepted evidence sources, 6 corroborating sources, and 6 major unresolved leads. The principal evidentiary limitation is the separation between institutional corruption or obstruction and personally attributable enrichment or criminal intent.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence

## 1. Orientation

Andrés Manuel López Obrador was Mexico’s president in 2023 and the dominant political actor in the federal executive and MORENA. His governing program was clear: the “Fourth Transformation,” anti-corruption, social transfers, state-led infrastructure, energy sovereignty, and reduced insecurity.

The 2023 record shows strong agenda-setting and substantial mobilization of federal resources, but uneven execution. Welfare and education budgets expanded, and the government opened an initial section of the Maya Train. At the same time, health-system restructuring, project delays, cost escalation, military expansion, and persistent insecurity exposed coordination and implementation weaknesses. Courts and Congress constrained constitutional change, while the administration’s preference for executive control and military implementation sometimes reduced transparency and independent review.

## 2. Compact evidence index

### WEB-8B-01 — The governing program was explicit and repeatedly operationalized

The Fifth Government Report and the National Development Plan framed AMLO’s program around the Fourth Transformation, social welfare, anti-corruption, state capacity, infrastructure, energy sovereignty, and public security. These goals were repeated in official speeches, budgets, and policy decisions.

Source: [Quinto Informe de Gobierno](https://amlo.presidente.gob.mx/5-informe-de-gobierno), Presidency of Mexico, 1 September 2023; [Plan Nacional de Desarrollo 2019–2024](https://www.gob.mx/cms/uploads/attachment/file/854385/PND_2019-2024.pdf).

Status: target-period and inherited program. Attribution: direct.

### WEB-8B-02 — Federal budgets translated welfare goals into large allocations

CONEVAL’s analysis of the 2023 budget found increases in several program areas: the Welfare Ministry’s social-program budget rose 39.5%; the old-age pension budget rose 42.6%; education programs such as La Escuela es Nuestra rose 93.7%; and the upper-secondary Benito Juárez scholarship rose 8.9%.

These figures show resource mobilization, but they are approved budgets, not proof of delivery or effectiveness.

Source: [Análisis de los programas sociales del PEF 2022–2023](https://www.coneval.org.mx/EvaluacionDS/MejorasUso/IPP/Documents/ANALISIS_PEF_2022-2023_IE.pdf), CONEVAL, 2023, pp. 7–13 and 21–22.

### WEB-8B-03 — Health implementation showed a gap between reform and capacity

CONEVAL recorded a 12.9% fall in the IMSS-BIENESTAR budget between 2022 and 2023, even as the government pursued health-system centralization and universalization. CONEVAL’s poverty report also found health-service deprivation at 39.1% in 2022, compared with 28.2% in 2020.

This indicates a major implementation risk: the government redesigned the health system while access indicators deteriorated. Causal attribution is not complete because the figures also reflect pandemic disruption and administrative transition.

### WEB-8B-04 — Poverty outcomes improved, but cannot be attributed solely to government action

CONEVAL reported that poverty declined from 43.9% in 2020 to 36.3% in 2022. This supported the administration’s claim of social progress. However, the improvement coincided with post-pandemic economic recovery, labor-market changes, remittances, and other factors. Social deprivation remained extensive, and health access worsened.

Source: [Medición multidimensional de la pobreza en México 2022](https://www.coneval.org.mx/Medicion/MP/Documents/MMP_2022/Pobreza_multidimensional_2022.pdf), CONEVAL, August 2023, pp. 9–14 and 21–24.

Status: 2022 outcome published during 2023. Attribution: moderate.

### WEB-8B-05 — The Maya Train demonstrated mobilization but weak delivery discipline

The Maya Train began partial passenger service in December 2023. Associated Press reported that the opening occurred amid incomplete sections, delays, and substantial cost escalation beyond initial projections. The project was a central AMLO priority and was placed under military-linked management.

This is evidence of strong presidential mobilization and agenda control, but also of weak cost, schedule, and completion management.

Source: [Mexico’s Maya tourist train opens for partial service amid delays and cost overruns](https://apnews.com/article/7b0db1b6b262acde6721debceaa5af7e), Associated Press, 16 December 2023; corroborating [ASF audit materials](https://www.asf.gob.mx/Trans/Informes/IR2023c/Documentos/Auditorias/2023_0143_a.pdf).

### WEB-8B-06 — Military and state-enterprise implementation expanded executive reach

The BTI report described the administration’s expansion of armed-forces responsibilities into internal security, customs, ports, infrastructure, and National Guard administration. This gave AMLO a large implementation network outside the traditional civilian bureaucracy.

The arrangement increased the executive’s operational reach but also created coordination, transparency, civilian-control, and accountability risks. It is evidence of mobilization, not by itself evidence of competent or successful delivery.

Source: [BTI 2024 Country Report: Mexico](https://bti-project.org/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_MEX.pdf), Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 2 and 8–10.

### WEB-8B-07 — Courts constrained the program when implementation exceeded constitutional limits

The Supreme Court invalidated the electoral Plan B reforms after finding serious procedural defects. This shows that AMLO’s ordinary legislative coalition could enact major changes but lacked unlimited authority and that judicial review remained an effective constraint.

The episode also shows a recurring implementation pattern: ambitious executive initiatives were sometimes pursued through accelerated or legally vulnerable procedures.

Source: [SCJN communiqué on invalidation of the electoral reform](https://www.internet2.scjn.gob.mx/red2/comunicados/noticia.asp?id=7408), Supreme Court of Mexico, 2023.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 Clear governing program — Strong evidence.** The Fourth Transformation, National Development Plan, government reports, budgets, and repeated speeches provided a clear program.
- **8B.2 Turning goals into machinery — Substantial evidence with weaknesses.** Budgets, welfare programs, health-system reform, infrastructure projects, and military assignments translated goals into machinery; legal and administrative vulnerabilities remained.
- **8B.3 Mobilizing the ruling system — Strong evidence.** The executive mobilized MORENA, federal agencies, the military, and state-linked enterprises around flagship priorities.
- **8B.4 Capable implementers — Mixed.** The government used military and loyal administrative networks effectively for rapid mobilization, but health, transparency, and infrastructure problems raise doubts about competence and institutional fit.
- **8B.5 Coordination and control — Mixed/adverse.** Federal reach was extensive, but delays, cost escalation, criminal territorial control, and uneven service delivery indicate incomplete coordination.
- **8B.6 From plans to practice — Mixed.** Welfare budgets and the Maya Train became concrete actions; health reform and some infrastructure projects showed major implementation gaps.
- **8B.7 Progress toward chosen goals — Mixed.** Poverty declined and transfers expanded, but insecurity remained high, health access worsened, and major projects underperformed on schedule and cost.
- **8B.8 Learning and correction — Inconclusive to weak.** CONEVAL and ASF generated usable evidence, but the inspected material does not show systematic correction proportional to documented failures.
- **8B.9 Managing crises and resistance — Mixed.** AMLO maintained political control despite courts, Congress, criminal groups, and administrative resistance, but often responded through centralization and military delegation rather than institutional repair.
- **8B.10 Durable goal achievement — Uncertain and mixed.** Some transfers, infrastructure, and institutional changes were durable; health-system restructuring, military expansion, and flagship projects remained vulnerable to reversal, legal challenge, or capacity problems.

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- Detailed 2023 execution reports for welfare, health, education, and infrastructure budgets.
- ASF audit findings and corrective-action records for the Maya Train and other flagship projects.
- IMSS-BIENESTAR implementation, staffing, procurement, and coverage data.
- Military infrastructure contracts and completion milestones.
- State-level implementation variation and the role of governors and MORENA networks.
- Evidence showing whether the administration changed policy after CONEVAL, ASF, or court findings.

Source count: 7 accepted evidence sources, 6 corroborating sources, and 6 major unresolved leads. The principal limitation is that several outcome indicators lag the target year, while official reports emphasize achievements and do not independently establish execution quality.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

## Continuation review: 1B Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility

I could not read or update `research-ledger-manifest.json`: the workspace was read-only and shell access failed before the ledger could be inspected. The records below therefore use continuation IDs and should be merged only after checking the existing manifest for collisions. No score is assigned.

### Updated orientation

Mexico had no nuclear weapons and was not an interstate belligerent in 2023. Its relevant catastrophic-risk responsibilities concerned peaceful nuclear regulation, safeguards, radioactive-source security, cyber defense of state systems, and surveillance technologies with serious civil-liberties and escalation implications.

The continuation improves the record in three areas:

- Mexico chaired the 2023 Second Meeting of States Parties to the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons and helped produce a declaration supporting complete elimination.
- Mexico’s nuclear regulator reported 2023 training and security activities, but the underlying annual report was not retrievable, so this remains a lead rather than accepted evidence.
- After the Guacamaya breach, official sources documented a Cyber Incident Response Team and cyber-defense functions. However, the record still does not establish the effectiveness of remediation, independent testing, or whether the breach caused operational compromise.

Pegasus evidence is technically strong for infections in 2019–2021 but does not prove a particular Mexican agency caused every infection. The 2023 controversy is relevant because AMLO publicly described military surveillance as “investigation” rather than espionage and defended the intelligence rationale.

## Evidence index and lens dispositions

- **1B.1 Reducing existential risk — Partial evidence.** WEB-1B-R1-001 supports diplomatic nuclear-risk reduction. Cyber-defense evidence is formal and procedural but does not prove effectiveness.
- **1B.2 Responsible nuclear rhetoric — Supported by official and diplomatic evidence.** Mexico’s 2023 TPNW leadership and anti-use declaration are favorable evidence. No reckless nuclear threats were found.
- **1B.3 Safe nuclear control — Gap remains.** CNSNS documentation indicates regulator activity, but the annual report could not be opened. No adequate evidence of command-and-control testing, emergency exercises, or enforcement was recovered.
- **1B.4 Arms control and inspections — Partial evidence.** Mexico participated actively in the TPNW and maintained inherited nonproliferation commitments. Evidence of 2023 inspection performance remains incomplete.
- **1B.5 Nuclear cover for aggression — No adverse evidence found.** Mexico did not use nuclear capability to shield aggression or repression. This is an exposure finding, not proof of positive safety performance.
- **1B.6 Preventing proliferation — Partial/inherited evidence.** Treaty participation and the nuclear regulator’s formal mandate support an inherited control framework; target-year enforcement evidence remains missing.
- **1B.7 Independent risk expertise — Partial favorable evidence.** Mexico’s TPNW presidency included a scientific advisory group with Mexican scientists. This demonstrates expert participation in diplomacy, not necessarily domestic independence.
- **1B.8 Crisis de-escalation — Partial favorable evidence.** Nuclear diplomacy was explicitly de-escalatory. Cyber and surveillance crises show public response and denial, but not a documented corrective process.
- **1B.9 Other catastrophic risks — Mixed.** Sedena and the National Guard maintained cyber-response structures, but the Guacamaya breach and lack of independent remediation evidence prevent a conclusion that cyber risk was managed effectively.
- **1B.10 Lasting risk posture — Unresolved.** Mexico’s diplomatic nuclear posture remained restraint-oriented, but the evidence is insufficient to determine whether domestic nuclear safety and cyber resilience were safer at the end of 2023.

## Source limitations

The strongest new technical source, Citizen Lab, establishes infections with high confidence but explicitly says the available technical data did not identify the responsible Mexican government customer. Official Sedena and presidential accounts establish institutional mandates and stated rationales, not operational adequacy. Nuclear evidence remains predominantly diplomatic and institutional; the missing CNSNS annual report prevents a reliable target-year assessment of inspections, emergency readiness, staffing, and enforcement.
